Improving the Regularity of Vector Fields
Brian Street, Liding Yao

Abstract
Let α > 0 \alpha>0 , β > α \beta>\alpha , and let X 1 , … , X q X_{1},\ldots,X_{q} be 𝒞 loc α \mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}} vector fields on a 𝒞 α + 1 \mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1} manifold which span the tangent space at every point, where 𝒞 s \mathscr{C}^{s} denotes the Zygmund-Hölder space of order s s . We give necessary and sufficient conditions for when there is a 𝒞 β + 1 \mathscr{C}^{\beta+1} structure on the manifold, compatible with its 𝒞 α + 1 \mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1} structure, with respect to which X 1 , … , X q X_{1},\ldots,X_{q} are 𝒞 loc β \mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}} . This strengthens previous results of the first author which dealt with the setting α > 1 \alpha>1 , β > max ⁡ { α , 2 } \beta>\max\{\alpha,2\} .



1
Introduction

Fix
\alpha>0
and let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}
be
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
vector fields on a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
manifold
\mathfrak{M}
of dimension
n
, which span the tangent space at every point, where

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
denotes the Zygmund-Hölder space of order
\alpha
.
1
1

1

For
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\mathscr{C}^{m+\alpha}
coincides with the usual Hölder space
C^{m,\alpha}
. For
\alpha\in\{0,1\}
, these spaces differ:
C^{m+1,0}\subsetneq C^{m,1}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{m+1}
.

In this paper, we investigate the following:

Question 1
.

Fix
\beta\in[\alpha,\infty)
. When is there a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}
manifold structure on
\mathfrak{M}
,
compatible with its
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
structure, respect to which
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
?

Question
1
is local in nature, so we focus instead on the following local version:

Question 2
.

Fix
\beta\in[\alpha,\infty)
and
x\in\mathfrak{M}
. When is there a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}

of
x
and a
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha+1}
diffeomorphism
\Phi:\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}U
, such that

\Phi^{*}X_{1},\ldots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
vector fields on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
?
Here,
\mathbb{B}^{n}
denotes the open unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We give necessary and sufficient conditions on
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}
for when Question
2
has an affirmative answer;
and therefore give necessary and sufficient conditions for when
Question
1
has an affirmative answer.

When
\alpha>1
and
\beta>2
, Questions
1
and
2

were completely answered in work of the first author and Stovall
[
SS18
,
Str21
,
Str20a
]
; which also proved stronger
quantitative results (see Section
1.3
for our distinction between quantitative and qualitative results).
In this paper, by focusing only on the qualitative Questions
1
and
2
we are able to prove results for all
\alpha>0
,
\beta\geq\alpha
,
and the proof is simpler. Our methods can also be used
to improve the quantitative results of
[
SS18
,
Str21
,
Str20a
]
:
see Section
9
.

1.1
Informal Statement of Results

Much of the difficulty in this paper comes from working with
\alpha
and
\beta
small.
In this section, we informally describe the results without worrying about such difficulties.

We begin with the case when
\beta=\infty
and
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}

are
C^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
vector fields on a

C^{2}
manifold
\mathfrak{M}
of dimension
n
, which span the tangent space at every point.
This is a special cases of results in
[
Str21
]
.

Because
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}
are
C^{1}
vector fields which span the tangent space at every point,
we may write

[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{q}c_{i,j}^{k}X_{k},\quad c_{i,j}^{k}:\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\text{ is continuous}.

Theorem 1.1

(
[
Str21
]
)
.

Fix
x\in\mathfrak{M}
. The following are equivalent:

1.

There is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
x
and a
C^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
diffeomoprhism

\Phi:\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}U
, such that
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\ldots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}

are
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}
vector fields on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

2.

The functions
c_{i,j}^{k}
can be chosen so that the following holds.
There is a neighborhood
V\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
x
such that
for every
L\in\mathbb{N}
and every list
l_{1},\ldots,l_{L}\in\{1,\ldots,q\}
, the functions

X_{l_{1}}X_{l_{2}}\cdots X_{l_{L}}c_{i,j}^{k}\big|_{V}:V\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
are continuous.
We will write this condition as
c_{i,j}^{k}\big|_{V}\in C^{\infty}_{X}(V)
.

In this paper,
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
vector fields
on a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
manifold,
\mathfrak{M}
. Informally, we wish our main result to
say that the following are equivalent:

1.

There is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
x
and a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
diffeomoprhism

\Phi:\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}U
, such that
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\ldots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}

are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
vector fields on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

2.

The
c_{i,j}^{k}
can be chosen such that
c_{j,k}^{l}\big|_{V}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X}(V)
for some neighborhood
V\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
x
, where
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X}(V)

is an appropriate Zygmund-Hölder space with respect to the vector fields
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}
.

When
\alpha>1
and
\beta>2
, this equivalence was proved in
[
Str21
]
; the main result of this paper gives an extension of this result to all
\beta\geq\alpha>0
. Unfortunately,
for
\alpha>0
small, the commutator of
[X_{j},X_{k}]
does not even immediately make sense
and many of the usual operations on
\mathfrak{M}
do not make immediate sense. Thus, much
of this paper is devoted to making sense of conditions similar to
2

in such low regularity. As we will see, this is a bit easier when
\alpha>1/2
and so our results
take a different form depending on whether
\alpha\in(0,1/2]
or
\alpha>1/2
.

1.2
Relation to Results of DeTurck and Kazdan

The results in this paper may be reminiscent of the celebrated results of DeTurck and Kazdan

[
DK81
]
regarding a coordinate system in which a Riemannian metric tensor has optimal regularity.
It seems that there are no direct implications between our results and their
results; however there are many similarities.
We present this in more detail in Section
8
;
there the following ideas are discussed.

DeTurck and Kazdan showed that a Riemannian metric tensor has optimal
regularity in harmonic coordinates
[
DK81
, Lemma 1.2]
.
Analogously, we present a natural Riemannian metric associated to
vector fields
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
(which form a basis of the tangent
space at every point)
such that
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
have optimal regularity in harmonic
coordinates with respect to this metric.

DeTurck and Kazdan also showed that a Riemannian metric tensor
may not have optimal regularity in geodesic normal
coordinates
[
DK81
, Example 2.3]
.
Analogously, we show that vector fields may not have optimal
regularity in canonical coordinates of the first kind.

However, the heart of our main result is not just to provide a coordinate
system in which vector fields have optimal regularity. Instead,
we provide a test to determine what that optimal regularity is.
This test can be carried out in any coordinate system and does
not require solving any differential equations.

Both this work and
[
DK81
]

use methods introduced by Malgrange
[
Mal69
]
.

Remark 1.2
.

It may be somewhat unexpected that vector fields may not have optimal
regularity in canonical coordinates of the first kind. Indeed,
there is a long history of writing vector fields in these coordinates
because they provide a coordinate system in which the vector fields
are often particularly easy to study. In the theory of Lie groups
this is classical (see, for example,
[
Che46
, page 115]
).
Outside of the setting of Lie groups, canonical coordinates have been
used in the quantitative study of sub-Riemannian geometry,
beginning with the work of Nagel, Stein, and Wainger
[
NSW85
]
,
and later used by Tao and Wright
[
TW03
]
,
the first author
[
Str11
]
,
Montanari and Morbidelli
[
MM12
]
,
and the first author and Stovall
[
SS18
]
, among others.
In
[
Str21
]
, the first author moved beyond canonical coordinates
to strengthen these theories. We see now that this is necessary:
sharp results like the ones in this paper and in
[
Str21
]

cannot be obtained using canonical coordinates.

Remark 1.3
.

If
X
is a nonzero
C^{1}
vector field on a one dimensional manifold
\mathfrak{M}
,
then the canonical coordinate system with respect to
X
, near the point
x_{0}
,
is the map
\Phi_{x_{0}}(t)=e^{tX}x_{0}
. Since
\Phi_{x_{0}}^{*}X=\partial_{t}
,
we see that canonical coordinate system do provide optimal regularity in this simple
setting. However, once we move to two dimensions, with two vector fields,
Lemma
8.6
shows that canonical coordinates may not
give the optimal regularity.

1.3
Qualitative versus Quantitative

Most of the results in this paper are
qualitative
in the following sense.
We give necessary and sufficient conditions so that a map
\Phi
as in Question
2

exists. If one traces through the proof, the
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
norms of the coefficients of

\Phi^{*}X_{1},\ldots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}
depend on (among other things) quantities like:

•

Upper bounds for
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
norms of the coefficients of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}
in some fixed coordinate system
near
x
.

•

A lower bound,
>0
, for the quantity:

\max_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}\in\{1,\ldots q\}}\left|\det\left(X_{j_{1}}(x)|\ldots|X_{j_{n}}(x)\right)\right|,

(1.1)

where in the above expression
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}
are written as column vectors in the same fixed coordinate system near
x
.

Unfortunately, both above quantities depend on the choice of the original coordinate system.
Thus, if the vector fields are given in a coordinate system where the above upper and lower bounds
are bad, the estimates our proof gives are bad; even if there exists a different (unknown) choice of coordinate
system where the above estimates are better. Thus, while our results are qualitatively optimal
(we give necessary and sufficient conditions for each
\beta
),
the estimates which follow from our proofs may be far from optimal unless one happens to know a good coordinate
system in which to write the vector fields in the first place.

In the papers
[
SS18
,
Str21
,
Str20a
]
, the first author and Stovall
give such estimates on
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\ldots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}
in terms of quantities which are invariant under arbitrary
C^{2}
diffeomorphisms (we call such estimates quantitative).
Thus, they do not depend on any choice of coordinate system.
This is useful for questions from partial differential equations and harmonic analysis, where

\Phi
can be used as a scaling map. Such scaling maps originated in the smooth setting
in the foundational work of Nagel, Stein, and Wainger
[
NSW85
]
and were later worked on
by Tao and Wright
[
TW03
]
,
the first author
[
Str11
]
, and in the
above-mentioned series of papers by the first author and Stovall
[
SS18
,
Str21
,
Str20a
]
. Similar scaling in a non-smooth
setting was studied by Montanari and Morbidelli
[
MM12
]
,
though they do not address questions like the ones in this paper.

In Section
9
, we use the main methods of this paper,
combined with the methods of
[
SS18
,
Str21
]

to improve the main quantitative result of
[
Str21
]
(and also the main
quantitative result of
[
Str20b
]
).

2
Function Spaces

To state our main result, we need to introduce several function spaces related to the classical
Zygmund-Hölder spaces. Because we are working in low regularity, some care is needed in the
definitions.

2.1
Classical Zygmund-Hölder spaces

In this section, we describe the classical Zygmund-Hölder spaces, and the corresponding
spaces on a manifold; see Section
4
for proofs of the results stated here.

In what follows,
U
will either be equal to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
or equal to an bounded open set with smooth boundary in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
–we will usually be interested in the case when
U
is either
\mathbb{R}^{n}
or
an open ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We define the Zygmund-Hölder space

\mathscr{C}^{s}(U):=\mathscr{B}^{s}_{\infty,\infty}(U)
, where
\mathscr{B}^{s}_{\infty,\infty}

denotes the classical Besov space (see,
[
Tri10
, Section 2.3]

for
\mathscr{B}^{s}_{\infty,\infty}(U)

when
U=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
[
Tri92
, Chapter 5]
or
[
Tri06
, Chapter 1.11]
when

U
is an bounded smooth domain).
2
2

2

Many results concerning
\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)
, where
U
is a bounded smooth domain, follow from the corresponding
results concerning
\mathscr{C}^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
via the theory described in
[
Tri92
, Chapter 5]
.

We similarly define the vector valued space
\mathscr{C}^{s}(U;\mathbb{R}^{m})
.
The space
\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)
has some particularly concrete characterizations:

Remark 2.1
.

For
U=\mathbb{R}^{n}
or
U
an bounded open set with smooth boundary in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have

(i)

s\in(0,2)
:
\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)
consists of those continuous functions
f:U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that the following norm is finite

\sup_{x\in U}|f(x)|+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in U\\
h\in\mathbb{R}^{n},h\neq 0\\
x+h,x+2h\in U\end{subarray}}|h|^{-s}|f(x+2h)-2f(x+h)+f(x)|.

Moreover, the above expression gives a norm equivalent to
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)}
.
See
[
Tri10
, Theorem 2.5.7 (ii)]
and
[
Tri10
, (3.4.2/6)]
.

(ii)

s\in(0,1)
:
\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)
consists of those continuous functions
f:U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that the following norm is finite

\sup_{x\in U}|f(x)|+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in U\\
x\neq y\end{subarray}}|x-y|^{-s}|f(x)-f(y)|.

Moreover, the above expression gives a norm equivalent to
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)}
.
See
[
Tri10
, Remark 2.2.2/3]
and
[
Tri10
, (3.4.2/6)]
.

(iii)

s\in(1,\infty]
:
\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)
consists of those continuous
f:U\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{n}
, such that

f,\partial_{x_{j}}f\in\mathscr{C}^{s-1}(U)
,
1\leq j\leq n
. We have

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)}\approx\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s-1}(U)}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|\partial_{x_{j}}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s-1}(U)}
.
See
[
Tri10
, Theorem 2.5.7 (ii)]
and
[
Tri10
, Theorem 3.3.5(i)]
.

(iv)

s\in(-\infty,0]
:
\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)
consists of those distributions
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(U)

such that
f=g_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{x_{j}}g_{j}
for some
g_{0},\ldots,g_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{s+1}(U)
.
We have
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)}\approx\inf\sum_{j=0}^{n}\|g_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s+1}(U)}
, where the
infimum is taken over all such choices of
g_{0},\ldots,g_{n}
.
When
U=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, this can be seen by letting
g_{0}=(I+\bigtriangleup)^{-1}f
and
g_{j}=-\partial_{x_{j}}(I+\bigtriangleup)^{-1}f
, for
j=1,\ldots n
. See
[
Tri10
, Theorem 2.3.8]
.

(v)

When
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
r\in(0,1)
, then

\mathscr{C}^{m+r}(U)=C^{m,r}(U)
, with equivalence of norms.
See
[
Tri06
, Theorem 1.118 (i)]
.
However, when
r\in\{0,1\}
these spaces differ.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
r,s\in\mathbb{R}
with
r+s>0
,
r\geq s
.
The product map
(f,g)\mapsto fg
can be defined as a continuous map
\mathscr{C}^{r}(U)\times\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)
.

Proof.

This is a special case of
[
Tri10
, Theorem 2.8.2(i)]
when
U=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
[
Tri10
, Theorem 3.3.2(ii)]
when
U
is bounded open set with smooth boundary.
∎

Definition 2.3
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set. For
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
\mathscr{C}^{s}(U;TU)
, to be the space of vector fields (with distribution
coefficients)

Y=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\partial_{x_{j}}
, where
a_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)
. We identify

Y
with the distribution
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathscr{C}^{s}(U;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and define

\|Y\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)}:=\|(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(U;\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set. For
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
\mathscr{C}^{s}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
,
to be the space of
k
-forms (with distribution coefficients)

\omega=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n}\omega_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{k}
,
where
\omega_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)
. We identify
\omega
with the
distribution
(\omega_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}})_{1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\ldots<i_{k}\leq n}
, and define

\|\omega\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(U)}:=\|(\omega_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(U;\mathbb{R}^{Q_{n,k}})},

where
Q_{n,k}=\dim{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set. For
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
\mathscr{C}^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
to be the space of distributions
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(U)
, such that
for every
x\in U
, there exists an open ball
U^{\prime}\subseteq U
containing
x
with

f\big|_{U^{\prime}}\in\mathscr{C}^{s}(U^{\prime})
. We similarly define
\mathscr{C}^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
and

\mathscr{C}^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
.

For
\alpha>0
, we define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
manifolds in the usual way: the transition
functions are assumed to be
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
(see
[
Str21
, Section 5.4]

for some comments on this). Such manifold are, in particular,
C^{1}
manifolds, and so it
makes sense to talk about, for example, vector fields on such manifolds.

Remark 2.6
.

On a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
manifold
\mathfrak{M}
, it makes sense to talk about functions
in
\mathscr{C}^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
for
s\in(-\alpha,\alpha+1]
, vector fields
in
\mathscr{C}^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
for
s\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
,
and
k
-forms in
\mathscr{C}^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
for
s\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
.
See Lemma
4.1
and Definition
4.2
.

Let
\iota_{Y}
denote the interior product with respect to the vector field
Y

and let
\mathrm{Lie}_{Y}
denote the Lie derivative with respect to
Y
.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
manifold for some

\alpha>0
.

(i)

For
\beta\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
, the map

(Y,\omega)\mapsto\iota_{Y}\omega
is a continuous map

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\beta}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k-1}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right).

(ii)

For
\beta\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
, the map

(\eta,\omega)\mapsto\eta\wedge\omega
is a continuous map

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{l}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\beta}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+l}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right).

(iii)

If
\alpha>1/2
and
\beta\in(-\alpha+1,\alpha]
, the map
\omega\mapsto d\omega
is continuous

\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right).

(iv)

For
\beta\in(-\alpha+1,\alpha+1]
, the map

(Y,f)\mapsto Yf=:\mathrm{Lie}_{Y}f

is continuous

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M}).

(v)

If
\alpha>1/2
, then for
\beta\in(-\alpha+1,\alpha]
, the map

(Y,Z)\mapsto[Y,Z]=:\mathrm{Lie}_{Y}Z

is continuous

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M}).

(vi)

If
\alpha>1/2
, then for
\beta\in(-\alpha+1,\alpha]
, the map

(Y,\omega)\mapsto d\iota_{Y}\omega+\iota_{Y}d\omega=:\mathrm{Lie}_{Y}\omega

is continuous

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M}).

As can be seen in Section
1.1
, our main results
are in terms of the commutators of vector fields: i.e., the Lie derivative of one
vector field with respect to another. Proposition
2.7

(v)
shows
that such Lie derivatives,
\mathrm{Lie}_{Y}
, only make sense
when
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
for
\alpha>1/2
. Because
of this, when
\alpha>1/2
, the characterizations in our main result can be made somewhat simpler.
However, it is still possible to make sense of some of these ideas when
\alpha\in(0,1/2]
,
as we now make precise.

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
\beta,\gamma\in(-\alpha,\alpha+1]
.
Let
U,V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be open and let
F:U\xrightarrow{\sim}V
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
diffeomorphism. Fix a
k
-form
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
.
Then, the following are equivalent:

(i)

d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
.

(ii)

d(F_{*}\theta)\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(V;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}V\right)
.

Moreover, in this case, for all
p\in U
, there is a neighborhood
V^{\prime}\subseteq V
of
F(p)

and
\tau\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(V^{\prime};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}V^{\prime}\right)
such that

d(F_{*}\theta)\big|_{V^{\prime}}=d\tau
.

For the remainder of this section, let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
manifold for some
\alpha>0
.

Definition 2.9
.

Let
\gamma\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
,
\beta\in[\gamma,\alpha+1]
, and
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
. We say
d\theta
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
,
if for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, there is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
coordinate system
F:V\xrightarrow{\sim}U
,
where
V
is a neighborhood of
p
and
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is open, such that
dF_{*}\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
.

Proposition
2.8
shows that
Definition
2.9
is well-defined: it does not depend on
the choice of the coordinate system
F
. However, we do not define the form
d\theta
itself:
we only define its regularity. Indeed, if
\beta-1\leq-\alpha
, the space

\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)

is not well-defined.
However, when
\beta-1>-\alpha
, the form
d\theta
is well-defined, as the next result shows.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\gamma\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
and
\beta\in(-\alpha+1,\alpha+1]
.
Suppose
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)

is such that
d\theta
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
.
Then,
d\theta
is given by a well-defined form in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
.
I.e., there is a unique form
\tau\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
such that in every coordinate system
F:V\xrightarrow{\sim}U
, where
V\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
and
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
are open, we have
F_{*}\tau=d(F_{*}\theta)
. Furthermore,
this form
\tau
is closed in the sense that in every such coordinate system, we have
dF_{*}\tau=0
.

Definition 2.11
.

For
\gamma\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
,
\beta\in(-\alpha+1,\alpha+1]
,
and
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
,
we write
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
to mean
d\theta
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
,
and we identify
d\theta
with the unique closed form
in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)

given in Lemma
2.10
.

Convention 2.12
.

For
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, we say
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta^{+}}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
, if
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
for some
\varepsilon>0
.

2.2
Zygmund-Hölder spaces with respect to vector fields

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
manifold for some
\alpha>0
, and let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
be
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
vector fields which span the tangent
space to
\mathfrak{M}
at every point.
Since
\mathfrak{M}
is only a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
manifold, it does not make sense to talk about whether a function on
\mathfrak{M}
has regularity higher than
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.
However, we can make sense of higher regularity with respect to the vector fields
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}
.

Definition 2.13

(
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}
-functions)
.

For
\beta>-\alpha
, we let
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
be the space of those functions
\mathfrak{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined recursively by:

•

If
\beta\in(-\alpha,1]
,
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M}):=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
.

•

If
\beta>1
,
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
consists of those
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})\bigcap C^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
such that

\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{j}}f=X_{j}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
, for
1\leq j\leq q
.

We can make a similar definition for vector fields and forms, so long as
\alpha>1/2
:

Definition 2.14

(
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}
-vector fields)
.

Suppose
\alpha>1/2
. For
\beta>-\alpha
, we let
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
be
be the space of those vector fields on
\mathfrak{M}
defined recursively by:

•

If
\beta\in(-\alpha,\frac{1}{2}]
,
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
.

•

If
\beta>\frac{1}{2}
,
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
consists
of those
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})\bigcap\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
such that

\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{j}}Y=[X_{j},Y]\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
, for
1\leq j\leq q
.

Definition 2.15

(
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}
-forms)
.

Suppose
\alpha>1/2
and
k\geq 1
. For
\beta>-\alpha
, we let
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)

be the space of those
k
-forms on
\mathfrak{M}
defined recursively by:

•

If
\beta\in(-\alpha,\frac{1}{2}]
,

\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
.

•

If
\beta>\frac{1}{2}
,
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
consists of those

\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)\bigcap\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
such that

\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{j}}\theta=(d\iota_{X_{j}}+\iota_{X_{j}}d)\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
,
for
1\leq j\leq q
.

When
\alpha\in(0,1/2]
, we cannot use Definition
2.15
. However, we can make an appropriate analog of Definition
2.9

with respect to the vector fields
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}
:

Definition 2.16

(
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}
for differentials)
.

Suppose
\alpha>0
.
Let
\beta>-\alpha
,
1\leq k\leq n-1
and let
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{(-\alpha)^{+}}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
.
We say
d\theta
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
if:

•

If
\beta\in(-\alpha,1]
, we assume
d\theta
has regularity

\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
.

•

If
\beta\in(1,2]
, we assume
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{0^{+}}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
and
\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{j}}d\theta=d\iota_{X_{j}}d\theta
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
,
for
1\leq j\leq q
.

•

If
\beta>2
, we assume
d\theta
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
,
and
\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{j}}d\theta=d\iota_{X_{j}}d\theta
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
,
for
1\leq j\leq q
.

Remark 2.17
.

Note that if
\beta\geq 0
, Definitions
2.13
and
2.16
do not depend on
\alpha
. Similarly, when
\beta\geq-\frac{1}{2}
, Definitions
2.14
and
2.15
do not depend on
\alpha
.

3
The Main Result

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
, and let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}
be
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}

vector fields on a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
manifold
\mathfrak{M}
of dimension
n
, which span the tangent space
at every point. Fix a point
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, and re-order
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}

so that
X_{1}(p),\ldots,X_{n}(p)
form a basis for
T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
.
Let
\lambda^{1},\ldots,\lambda^{n}
be the dual basis for
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
, defined
on a neighborhood of
p
. Then, the following are equivalent:

(a)

There is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
and a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
diffeomorphism
\Phi:\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}U
,
such that
\Phi(0)=p
and
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\ldots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})
.

(b)

There is a neighborhood
U
of
p
such that for
1\leq j\leq n
,

d\lambda^{j}
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
, and
for
1\leq j\leq n
,
n+1\leq k\leq q
,
\langle\lambda^{j},X_{k}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
.

If
\alpha>1/2
, then in addition we have the following equivalent conditions:

3.

There is a neighborhood
U
of
p
such that
for
1\leq j\leq n
,
\lambda^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U,T^{*}U\right)
, and for
1\leq j\leq n
,
n+1\leq k\leq q
,
\langle\lambda^{j},X_{k}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
.

4.

There is a neighborhood
U
of
p
such that for
1\leq j\leq q
,

X_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;TU\right)
.

Remark 3.2
.

(a)

\Leftrightarrow

4
is the conclusion alluded to in Section
1.1
.

4
Classical Function Spaces, Revisited

In this section, we prove the basic results we require about Zygmund-Hölder spaces. In particular, we prove the results from Section
2
. We begin by discussing the main
result we need to help understand
the various objects under consideration on
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifolds.

Lemma 4.1
.

Fix
\alpha>0
,
let
U,V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be open sets, and let

\Phi:U\xrightarrow{\sim}V
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
diffeomorphism.

(i)

For
\beta\in(-\alpha,\alpha+1]
and

f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V)
,

f\circ\Phi

is defined as a distribution and we have
f\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
.

(ii)

For
\beta\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
and
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V;TV)
,
\Phi^{*}X

is defined and

\Phi^{*}X\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
.

(iii)

For
\beta\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
and

\omega\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(V;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}V\right)
, then
\Phi^{*}\omega
is defined
and
\Phi^{*}\omega\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)

Proof.

We give
U
coordinates
x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}
and
V
coordinates

y^{1},\ldots,y^{n}
.
We begin with
(i)
. For
\beta>0
,

f
is a continuous function and the regularity
of
f\circ\Phi
is classical. See
[
Str21
, Lemma 5.15]

for a discussion of this classical case.
For nonpositive
\beta
, we proceed by induction.
We prove the result for
\beta\in(-l+1,-l]\cap(-\alpha,\alpha+1]
,
for
l=-1,0,1,2,\ldots
. The base case,
l=-1
, follows
from the above discussion for
\beta>0
. For
l\in\mathbb{N}
,
we assume the result for
l-1
and prove it for
l
.

Fix a point
y_{0}\in V
, and let
B_{y_{0}}\subseteq V
be an open ball
centered at
y_{0}
with
\overline{B_{y_{0}}}\subseteq V
.
By Remark
2.1

(iv)
, we may write

f=g_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{y^{j}}g_{j}
, where

g_{0},\ldots,g_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(B_{y_{0}})
.
Letting
\Psi=(\psi_{1},\ldots\psi_{n}):=\Phi^{-1}
, we have

(\partial_{y^{j}}g_{j})\circ\Phi=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\partial_{x^{j}}(g_{j}\circ\Phi)\right)\left(\left(\partial_{y^{j}}\psi_{k}\right)\circ\Phi\right).

By the already proved case
\beta=\alpha
, we have

\left(\partial_{y^{k}}\psi_{k}\right)\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Psi(B_{y_{0}}))
and by the inductive
hypothesis
\partial_{x^{k}}(g_{j}\circ\Phi)\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Psi(B_{y_{0}}))
.
Also, by the inductive hypothesis
g_{0}\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Psi(B_{y_{0}}))\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Psi(B_{y_{0}}))
. Using Lemma
2.2
, we conclude

f\circ\Phi=g_{0}\circ\Phi+\sum(\partial_{y^{j}}g_{j})\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Psi(B_{y_{0}}))
. It is easy to see
that the distribution obtained in this way does not depend on
the choice of
g_{0},\ldots,g_{n}
with
f=g_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{y^{j}}g_{j}
.
Since
y_{0}\in V
was arbitrary, this completes the proof of
(i)
.

For
(ii)
, write

X=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}\partial_{y^{j}}
, where
a_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V)
. Then, if
\Psi=(\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{n})=\Phi^{-1}
,

\Phi^{*}X=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(a_{j}\circ\Phi\right)\left((\partial_{y^{j}}\psi_{k})\circ\Phi\right)\partial_{x^{k}}.

By
(i)
,

a_{j}\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
,
and
(\partial_{x^{j}}\phi_{k})\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. Since
\beta\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
, by hypothesis,
Lemma
2.2
implies

\Phi^{*}X\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
.

The proof of
(iii)
is very
similar to the proof of
(ii)
,
and follows easily by combining
(i)

with Lemma
2.2
. We leave the details to the reader.
∎

Lemma
4.1

establishes Remark
2.6
:
On a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
manifold
\mathfrak{M}
, it makes sense to talk about functions
in
\mathscr{C}^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
for
s\in(-\alpha,\alpha+1]
, vector fields in
\mathscr{C}^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
for
s\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
,
and
k
-forms in
\mathscr{C}^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
for
s\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
.
This is because these properties are invariant under
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
diffeomorphisms. By a similar proof,
one can show that the more general concept of a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
tensor
is well-defined for
\beta\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
, though the only
tensors we use in this paper are vector fields and forms.

For completeness we put the definition of functions, vector fields and differential forms on manifold as below,
which is the obvious analog of
the standard definitions (see, for example,
[
Hör03
, Definition 6.3.3]
):

Definition 4.2
.

Let
\alpha>0
,
\beta\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
and
\gamma\in(-\alpha,\alpha+1]
. Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
n
-dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifold equipped with
the maximal
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-atlas
\mathscr{A}=\{\phi:U_{\phi}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
. Namely, each
\phi\in\mathscr{A}
is a homeomorphism
\phi:U_{\phi}\xrightarrow{\sim}\phi(U_{\phi})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
;
\phi\circ\psi^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\psi(U_{\psi}\cap U_{\phi});\mathbb{R}^{n})
whenever
\phi,\psi\in\mathscr{A}
satisfy
U_{\psi}\cap U_{\phi}\neq\varnothing
; and
\mathscr{A}
is maximal with these properties.

•

A
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-function is a collection
f=\{f_{\phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\phi(U_{\phi}))\}_{\phi\in\mathscr{A}}
, such that

f_{\phi}=f_{\psi}\circ(\psi\circ\phi^{-1}),\quad\text{on }\phi(U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi})\text{ whenever }U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi}\neq\varnothing.

•

A
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-vector field is a collection
X=\{X_{\phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\phi(U_{\phi});T\mathbb{R}^{n})\}_{\phi\in\mathscr{A}}
, such that

X_{\phi}=(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}X_{\psi},\quad\text{on }\phi(U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi})\text{ whenever }U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi}\neq\varnothing.

(4.1)

•

Let
1\leq k\leq n
. A
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}

k
-form is a collection
\omega=\left\{\omega_{\phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\phi(U_{\phi});{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\right\}_{\phi\in\mathscr{A}}
, such that

\omega_{\psi}=(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}\omega_{\phi},\quad\text{on }\phi(U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi})\text{ whenever }U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi}\neq\varnothing.

(4.2)

Remark 4.3
.

By Lemma
4.1
we are able to pullback functions, vector fields, and differential forms using
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-transition maps. Thus, the above objects are well-defined.

To prove Proposition
2.7
we use the following:

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
\alpha>0
, let
U,V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two open sets and let
\Phi:U\xrightarrow{\sim}V
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism.

(i)

For
\beta\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
, if
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V;TV)
and
\omega\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(V;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}V\right)
then
\Phi^{*}(\iota_{Y}\omega)=\iota_{\Phi^{*}Y}\Phi^{*}\omega
, and their common value is in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k-1}T^{*}U\right)
.

(ii)

For
\beta\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
, if
\sigma\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(V;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{l}T^{*}V\right)
and
\omega\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(V;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}V\right)
then
\Phi^{*}(\sigma\wedge\omega)=\Phi^{*}\sigma\wedge\Phi^{*}\omega
, and their common value is in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+l}T^{*}U\right)
.

(iii)

For
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
and
\beta\in(1-\alpha,\alpha]
, if
\omega\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(V;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}V\right)
then
\Phi^{*}d\omega=d\Phi^{*}\omega
, and their common value is in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
.

(iv)

For
\beta\in(1-\alpha,\alpha+1]
, if
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V;TV)
and
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V)
then
\Phi^{*}(Yf)=(\Phi^{*}Y)(\Phi^{*}f)
, and their common value is in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
.

(v)

If
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
and
\beta\in(1-\alpha,\alpha]
, if
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V;TV)
and
Z\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V;TV)
then
\Phi^{*}[Y,Z]=[\Phi^{*}Y,\Phi^{*}Z]
, and their common value is in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
.

(vi)

If
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
and
\beta\in(1-\alpha,\alpha]
, if
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V;TV)
and
\omega\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(V;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}V\right)
then
\Phi^{*}\mathrm{Lie}_{Y}\omega=\mathrm{Lie}_{\Phi^{*}Y}\Phi^{*}\omega
, and their common value is in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
.

Proof.

The formal computations are standard in differential geometry. What we need to be careful is that the products and compositions are defined, due to the low regularity of the objects involved.

We only prove
(i)
and
(iii)
, since the arguments for the others are similar.
We endow
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
with the standard coordinate system
(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
, and write
\Phi=:(\phi^{1},\dots,\phi^{n})
where
\phi^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
.

(i)
: We write
Y=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
and
\omega_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}:=\omega(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j_{1}}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j_{k}}})
for
1\leq j_{1},\dots,j_{k}\leq n
. By Lemma
2.2
,
a^{i}\omega_{ij_{1}\dots j_{k-1}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V)
and therefore

\iota_{Y}\omega=\frac{1}{(k-1)!}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j_{1},\dots,j_{k-1}=1}^{n}a^{i}\omega_{ij_{1}\dots j_{k-1}}dx^{j_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{j_{k-1}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k-1}T^{*}U\right).

(4.3)

Note that
\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi^{i}}=\Phi^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
,
i=1,\dots,n
, are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector fields on
U
that satisfy
3
3

3

We write
\delta_{i}^{j}
for the Kronecker delta functions (see (
5.6
)).

d\phi^{j}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi^{i}})=\frac{\partial\phi^{j}}{\partial\phi^{i}}=\delta_{i}^{j}
for
1\leq i,j\leq n
. By Lemma
4.1

(i)
,
a^{i}\circ\Phi,\omega_{ij_{1}\dots j_{k-1}}\circ\Phi,(a^{i}\omega_{ij_{1}\dots j_{k-1}})\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
are all defined. Therefore we have the following, where all the products and compositions are defined.

\displaystyle\iota_{\Phi^{*}Y}(\Phi^{*}\omega)=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{j_{0},\dots,j_{k-1}=1}^{n}\iota_{\sum_{i=1}^{n}(a^{i}\circ\Phi)\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi^{i}}}\left((\omega_{j_{0}\dots j_{k-1}}\circ\Phi)\cdot d\phi^{j_{0}}\wedge\dots\wedge d\phi^{j_{k-1}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j_{0},j_{1},\dots,j_{k-1}=1}^{n}\sum_{\rho=0}^{k-1}(a^{i}\circ\Phi)\cdot(\omega_{j_{0}\dots\widehat{j_{\rho}}\dots j_{k}}\circ\Phi)\cdot(-1)^{\rho}\frac{\partial\phi^{j_{\rho}}}{\partial\phi^{i}}d\phi^{j_{0}}\wedge\dots\wedge d\phi^{j_{\rho-1}}\wedge d\phi^{j_{\rho+1}}\wedge\dots\wedge d\phi^{j_{k-1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(k-1)!}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j_{1},\dots,j_{k-1}=1}^{n}((a^{i}\omega_{j_{1}\dots j_{k-1}})\circ\Phi)\cdot d\phi^{j_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge d\phi^{j_{k-1}}=\Phi^{*}(\iota_{Y}\omega).

The equality holds in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(V;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k-1}T^{*}V\right)
, completing the proof.

(iii)
: By passing to linear combinations it suffices to consider the form
\omega=fdx^{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{i_{k}}
where
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V)
.

By Lemma
4.1
,
f\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
and
\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{j}}\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
.
Since we also have
d\phi^{i_{1}},\dots,d\phi^{i_{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
, Lemma
2.2
shows that all below products are defined.
We have,

\Phi^{*}d(fdx^{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{i_{k}})=\Phi^{*}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{j}}dx^{j}\wedge dx^{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{i_{k}}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{j}}\circ\Phi\Big)d\phi^{j}\wedge d\phi^{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge d\phi^{i_{k}},

(4.4)

\begin{split}&d\Phi^{*}(fdx^{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{i_{k}})=d(f\circ\Phi)\wedge d\phi^{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge d\phi^{i_{k}}=\sum_{l=1}^{n}\frac{\partial(f\circ\Phi)}{\partial x^{l}}\wedge d\phi^{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge d\phi^{i_{k}}\\
&=\sum_{l,j=1}^{n}\Big(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{j}}\circ\Phi\Big)\frac{\partial\phi^{j}}{\partial x^{l}}dx^{l}\wedge d\phi^{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge d\phi^{i_{k}}.\end{split}

(4.5)

Since
\sum_{l=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\phi^{j}}{\partial x^{l}}dx^{l}=d\phi^{j}
, we have that
(
4.4
) and
(
4.5
) are equal,
completing the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.7
.

We only prove
(i)
, since the arguments for the other parts are similar.

By Definition
4.2
, we can write
Y=\{Y_{\phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\phi(U_{\phi});T\mathbb{R}^{n})\}_{\phi\in\mathscr{A}}
and
\omega=\left\{\omega_{\phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\phi(U_{\phi});{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\right\}_{\phi\in\mathscr{A}}
, where
\mathscr{A}
is the maximal
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-atlas for
\mathfrak{M}
.

For each
\phi\in\mathscr{A}
, by applying Lemma
2.2
on the coordinate components of
Y_{\phi}
and
\omega_{\phi}
, we see that the map
(Y_{\phi},\omega_{\phi})\mapsto\iota_{Y_{\phi}}\omega_{\phi}
is a continuous map
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\phi(U_{\phi});T\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\phi(U_{\phi});{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\phi(U_{\phi});{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k-1}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
.

By (
4.1
) and (
4.2
) we have
(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}Y_{\psi}=Y_{\phi}
and
(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}\omega_{\psi}=\omega_{\phi}
on
\phi(U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi})
.
By Lemma
4.4

(i)
we see that

(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}(\iota_{Y_{\psi}}\omega_{\psi})=\iota_{(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}Y_{\psi}}\left((\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}\omega_{\psi}\right)=\iota_{Y_{\phi}}\omega_{\phi},\quad\text{on }\phi(U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi}),\text{ whenever }U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi}\neq\varnothing.

Therefore
\{\iota_{Y_{\phi}}\omega_{\phi}\}_{\phi\in\mathscr{A}}
is a collection of
(k-1)
-forms satisfying (
4.2
), and therefore defines a
(k-1)
-form on
\mathfrak{M}
, which is denoted by
\iota_{Y}\omega
.

Finally, the continuity of
(Y,\omega)\mapsto\iota_{Y}\omega
comes from the fact that
(Y_{\phi},\omega_{\phi})\mapsto\iota_{Y_{\phi}}\omega_{\phi}
is continuous for each
\phi\in\mathscr{A}
.
∎

We now turn to the proofs of Proposition
2.8
and Lemma
2.10
.
For these, we require several standard objects and results.

Notation 4.5
.

We use the co-differential,
\vartheta=\vartheta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, which is a linear operator
taking
k
forms to
k-1
forms, satisfying for
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n
,

\vartheta\left(fdx^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{i_{k}}\right)=\sum_{l=1}^{k}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i_{l}}}(-1)^{l}dx^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{i_{l-1}}\wedge dx^{i_{l+1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{i_{k}}.

In particular on 1-forms,
-\vartheta
is the divergence operator, namely

\text{For }\theta=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta_{i}dx^{i},\quad\vartheta\theta=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\theta_{i}}{\partial x^{i}}.

For any form
\omega
, we have
d(\vartheta\omega)+\vartheta(d\omega)=\bigtriangleup\omega
, where

\bigtriangleup=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{x^{i}}^{2}
is the positive Laplacian acting on the components of
\omega
; in this
setting
\bigtriangleup
is called the Hodge Laplacian.

We will often convolve functions with
k
-forms.
Formally, if
\omega=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n}\omega_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}dx^{i_{k}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{i_{k}}
is a
k
-form, and
\phi
is a function, we set
\phi*\omega=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n}\left(\phi*\omega_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}\right)dx^{i_{k}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{i_{k}}
.

We will make use of the classical Newtonian potential.
Let

\mathcal{G}(x):=\begin{cases}-\frac{|x|}{2},&n=1,\\
-\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x|,&n=2,\\
|\mathbb{S}^{n-1}|^{-1}|x|^{2-n},&n\geq 3.\end{cases}

(4.6)

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\omega=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n}\omega_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}dx^{i_{k}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{i_{k}}
be a
k
-form
where each
\omega_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
is a compactly supported distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Then,
\sigma:=\mathcal{G}*\omega
is a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying
\bigtriangleup\sigma=\omega
.
Moreover, if for some open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\omega\big|_{U}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\beta}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
, then
\sigma\big|_{U}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\beta+2}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
.

Proof.

The convolution
\mathcal{G}*\omega
makes sense because
\omega_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}
is a compactly
supported distribution and
\mathcal{G}
is a distribution.
Since
\mathcal{G}
is well-known to be a fundamental solution for
the Laplacian
\bigtriangleup
, we have
\bigtriangleup(\mathcal{G}\ast\omega)=(\bigtriangleup\mathcal{G})*\omega=\omega
.
Since
\bigtriangleup\sigma=\omega
, the classical interior regularity for elliptic
equations shows that if
\omega\big|_{U}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\beta}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
, then
\sigma\big|_{U}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\beta+2}(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U)
; see, for example,
[
Tay11
, Proposition 4.1]
.
∎

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
0\leq k\leq n
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
and let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded open set, then there is a
C_{U,\gamma}>0
such that
\|\mathcal{G}\ast\omega\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+2}(U;\wedge^{k}T^{*}U)}\leq C_{U,\gamma}\|\omega\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(U;\wedge^{k}T^{*}U)}
for all compactly supported
k
-forms
\omega\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\gamma}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
.

Proof.

Set
\mathscr{X}
to be the completion of
\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\gamma}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
under the
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
-norm. Thus,
\mathscr{X}
is a closed subspace of
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
and
\|\omega\|_{\mathscr{X}}=\|\omega\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}
for all
\omega\in\mathscr{X}
.

When
\omega\in\mathscr{X}
, we have that
\operatorname{supp}\omega\subseteq\overline{U}
so
\omega\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{c}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
. By Lemma
4.6
,
\mathcal{G}\ast\omega\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+2}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
is well-defined. By restricting it to
U
we get
(\mathcal{G}\ast\omega)\big|_{U}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+2}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
.

By the closed graph theorem we have
\|\mathcal{G}\ast\omega\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+2}(U;\wedge^{k}T^{*}U)}\leq C\|\omega\|_{\mathscr{X}}=C\|\omega\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(U;\wedge^{k}T^{*}U)}
for some
C
that does not depend on
\omega
.

Therefore, for the same constant
C
we have
\|\mathcal{G}\ast\omega\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+2}(U;\wedge^{k}T^{*}U)}\leq C\|\omega\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(U;\wedge^{k}T^{*}U)}
for all
\omega\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\gamma}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
0\leq k\leq n
,
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
,
\beta>\gamma-1
and let
U^{\prime}\Subset U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two open sets.
4
4

4

Here, and in the rest of the paper,
A\Subset B
denotes that
A
is a relatively compact subset of
B
.

Suppose
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U,{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
satisfies
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U,{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
.
Then, there exist
\rho\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U^{\prime};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
and
\xi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U^{\prime};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k-1}T^{*}U^{\prime}\right)
such that

\theta\big|_{U^{\prime}}=\rho+d\xi.

Note that the case
\beta\leq\gamma-1
holds automatically if we pick
\rho:=\theta
and
\xi:=0
.

Proof.

Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
satisfy
\chi\equiv 1
on a neighborhood
over
\overline{U^{\prime}}
.
Define

\widehat{\rho}:=\mathcal{G}*\vartheta d(\chi\theta),\quad\widehat{\xi}:=\mathcal{G}*\vartheta(\chi\theta),\qquad\rho:=\widehat{\rho}\big|_{U^{\prime}},\quad\xi:=\widehat{\xi}\big|_{U^{\prime}}.

Since
\chi\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
, Lemma
4.6

shows
\widehat{\rho}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
and
\widehat{\xi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k-1}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
. Thus,
\xi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U^{\prime};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k-1}T^{*}U^{\prime}\right)
.
Also,
\bigtriangleup\widehat{\rho}\big|_{U^{\prime}}=\vartheta d(\chi\theta)\big|_{U^{\prime}}=\vartheta d\theta\big|_{U^{\prime}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\beta-2}
, by hypothesis.
Thus, by the interior regularity of elliptic PDEs (see
[
Tay11
, Proposition 4.1]
), we have
\rho=\widehat{\rho}\big|_{U^{\prime}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
. We also have,

\theta\big|_{U^{\prime}}=\bigtriangleup(\mathcal{G}*(\chi\theta))\big|_{U^{\prime}}=(\vartheta d+d\vartheta)(\mathcal{G}*(\chi\theta))\big|_{U^{\prime}}=\mathcal{G}*\vartheta d(\chi\theta)\big|_{U^{\prime}}+d\mathcal{G}*\vartheta(\chi\theta)\big|_{U^{\prime}}=\widehat{\rho}\big|_{U^{\prime}}+d\widehat{\xi}\big|_{U^{\prime}}=\rho+d\xi,

as desired.
∎

Finally, we require paraproduct decompositions.
Let
\psi_{0}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a Schwartz function whose
Fourier transform,
\hat{\psi}_{0}(\xi)=\int\psi_{0}(x)e^{2\pi ix\cdot\xi}\>dx
,
satisfies
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\psi}_{0}\subseteq\{\xi:|\xi|<8/3\}
and

\hat{\psi}_{0}(\xi)\equiv 1
for
|\xi|\leq 3/2
. Set

\psi_{j}(x):=\begin{cases}2^{nj}\psi_{0}(2^{j}x)&j>0,\\
0&j\leq-1.\end{cases}

Associated to
\psi_{0}
, we define two bilinear operators each taking a

k
form
\sigma
and an
l
-form
\omega

and outputting a
(k+l)
-form,

\mathfrak{P}(\sigma,\omega):=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left((\psi_{j}-\psi_{j-1})*\sigma\right)\wedge(\psi_{j-2}*\omega),\quad\mathfrak{R}(\sigma,\omega):=\sum_{|j-k|\leq 1}\left((\psi_{j}-\psi_{j-1})*\sigma\right)\wedge\left((\psi_{k}-\psi_{k-1})*\omega\right).

Lemma 4.9
.

We have the following properties of
\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathfrak{R}
. Fix
k,l\in\{0,\ldots n\}
.

(i)

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathfrak{P}
defines a continuous
bilinear map
\mathfrak{P}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\times L^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{l}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+l}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
.

(ii)

For
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
\beta<0
,
\mathfrak{P}
defines a continuous
bilinear map
\mathfrak{P}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{l}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\beta}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+l}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
.

(iii)

For
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
with
\alpha+\beta>0
,

\mathfrak{R}
defines a continuous bilinear map

\mathfrak{R}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{l}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\beta}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+l}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
.

(iv)

\sigma\wedge\omega=\mathfrak{P}(\sigma,\omega)+(-1)^{kl}\mathfrak{P}(\omega,\sigma)+\mathfrak{R}(\omega,\sigma)
holds
for
\sigma\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)

and
\omega\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{l}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
, where
\alpha+\beta>0
.

(v)

\mathfrak{P}
satisfies
d\mathfrak{P}(\sigma,\omega)=\mathfrak{P}(d\sigma,\omega)+(-1)^{k}\mathfrak{P}(\sigma,d\omega)
, for
k
-forms
\sigma
and
l
-forms
\omega
.

Proof.

For
0
forms,
(i)
and
(ii)
can be found in
[
BCD11
, Theorem 2.82]

and
(iii)
can be found in

[
BCD11
, Theorem 2.85]
.
By passing to their coordinate components we obtain the results for arbitrary forms.

(iv)
follows easily from the fact that

\sum_{j=0}^{N}(\psi_{j}-\psi_{j-1})*\omega=\psi_{N}*\omega\xrightarrow{N\rightarrow\infty}\omega
.
(v)
follows directly from the definitions.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.8
.

Let
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)

be such that
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
. We will show, for any point
p\in U
, there is a neighborhood

V^{\prime}\subseteq V
of
F(p)
and
\tau\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(V^{\prime};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}V^{\prime}\right)
, with
d(F_{*}\theta)\big|_{V^{\prime}}=d\tau
. This will
prove
(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii)
and the existence of
\tau
as claimed in the proposition. The reverse implication follows by reversing the roles
of
F_{*}\theta
and
\theta
.

Let
U^{\prime}\Subset U
be an open neighborhood of
p
.
By Lemma
4.8
, there are
\rho\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U^{\prime},{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U^{\prime}\right)
and
\xi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U^{\prime},{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k-1}T^{*}U^{\prime}\right)
such that
\theta\big|_{U^{\prime}}=\rho+d\xi
. By Lemma
4.4

(iii)
,
dF_{*}d\xi=d^{2}F_{*}\xi=0
, so

dF_{*}\theta\big|_{F(U^{\prime})}=dF_{*}\rho\big|_{F(U^{\prime})}+dF_{*}d\xi\big|_{F(U^{\prime})}=dF_{*}\rho\big|_{F(U^{\prime})}.

(4.7)

Since
\rho\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U^{\prime},{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U^{\prime}\right)
and
F
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
diffeomorphism, Lemma
4.1
shows
F_{*}\rho\in\mathscr{C}^{\min\{\alpha,\beta\}}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(F(U^{\prime}),{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
. If
\beta\leq\alpha
,
then we have
dF_{*}\rho\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
,
completing the proof with
V^{\prime}:=F(U^{\prime})
and
\tau:=F_{*}\rho
.

However, if
\beta>\alpha
, this does not imply the desired result.
To show
dF_{*}\rho\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
near
F(p)
, we construct a new
k
-form
\tau\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
such that
dF_{*}\rho=d\tau
near
F(p)
. The construction requires paraproducts.

Let
U^{\prime\prime}\Subset U^{\prime}
be a smaller open neighborhood of
p
.
We claim that there exist
\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})

and
\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k-1}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
, with compact supports and such that

F_{*}\rho\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n}\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}d\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}.

(4.8)

Write
\rho=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n}\rho_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}dx^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{i_{k}}
and denote
\Phi:=F^{-1}:V\xrightarrow{\sim}U
. Take
\chi_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(F(U^{\prime}))
such that
\chi_{1}\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}\equiv 1
.
Define

(\tilde{\phi}^{1},\ldots,\tilde{\phi}^{n}):=\chi_{1}\Phi
, so that each
\tilde{\phi}^{j}

is compactly supported and
\tilde{\phi}^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Let
\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}:=\chi_{1}(\rho_{i_{1}\ldots_{k}}\circ\Phi)\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
We have
\rho_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\circ\Phi\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}=\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}
and
\phi^{j}\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}=\tilde{\phi}^{j}\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}
, so

F_{*}\rho\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n}\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}d\tilde{\phi}^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\tilde{\phi}^{i_{k}}\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}.

(4.9)

Using Lemma
4.6
, we set
\mu^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}:=\mathcal{G}*\vartheta(d\tilde{\phi}^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\tilde{\phi}^{i_{k}})\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha+1}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k-1}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
. Since
d\tilde{\phi}^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\tilde{\phi}^{i_{k}}
is closed, we have

\begin{split}&d\mu^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}=d\vartheta\mathcal{G}*(d\tilde{\phi}^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\tilde{\phi}^{i_{k}})+\vartheta\mathcal{G}*d(d\tilde{\phi}^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\tilde{\phi}^{i_{k}})\\
&=\bigtriangleup\mathcal{G}*(d\tilde{\phi}^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\tilde{\phi}^{i_{k}})=d\tilde{\phi}^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\tilde{\phi}^{i_{k}}.\end{split}

(4.10)

Setting
\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}:=\chi_{1}\mu^{i_{1}\cdots i_{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k-1}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
,
(
4.8
) follows by combining
(
4.9
) and (
4.10
).

Define

\tau:=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n}\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},d\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})+(-1)^{k}\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots,i_{k}},d\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})+\mathfrak{R}(\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},d\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}).

(4.11)

We will show
\tau\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)

and
d\tau\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}=dF_{*}\rho\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}
; this will complete
the proof with
V^{\prime}:=F(U^{\prime\prime})
and we have used (
4.7
).

We turn to showing
d\tau\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}=dF_{*}\rho\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}
.
Since
\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}
is a
(k-1)
-form,
Lemma
4.9

(v)

shows
d\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})=\mathfrak{P}(d\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})+(-1)^{k-1}\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},d\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})
. Applying differential to both sides of this equation
and using
d^{2}=0
, we obtain

d\mathfrak{P}(d\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})=(-1)^{k}d\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},d\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}).

(4.12)

Using Lemma
4.9

(iv)
in the case
l=0
, (
4.12
), and (
4.8
), we have

\begin{split}&d\tau\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n}d\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},d\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})+(-1)^{k}d\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},d\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})+d\mathfrak{R}(\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},d\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}\\
&=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n}d\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},d\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})+d\mathfrak{P}(d\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})+d\mathfrak{R}(\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},d\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}\\
&=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n}d\left(\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}d\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}\right)\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})}\\
&=dF_{*}\rho\big|_{F(U^{\prime\prime})},\end{split}

as desired.

Finally, we show
\tau\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
, which will complete the proof.
Using that
\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
d\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\subset L^{\infty}
,
Lemma
4.9

(i)

shows
\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},d\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)

and Lemma
4.9

(iii)

shows
\mathfrak{R}(\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},d\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\beta}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
.

Thus, the proof will be complete once we show
\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},d\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
.
If
\beta>1
, then
d\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}\in L^{\infty}
.
Using that
\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
,
Lemma
4.9

(i)
then
implies
\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},d\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
.
If
\beta\leq 1
, then
d\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1-\alpha}
. Since
\beta-1-\alpha<0

and
\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
,
Lemma
4.9

(ii)

shows
\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\mu}^{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}},d\tilde{\rho}_{i_{1}\ldots i_{k}})\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1+\beta-1-\alpha}=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2.10
.

Let
\mathscr{A}=\{\phi:U_{\phi}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
be the
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-atlas of
\mathfrak{M}
. By Definition
4.2
,
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
is the collection
\Big\{\theta_{\phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U_{\phi};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U_{\phi}\right)\Big\}_{\phi\in\mathscr{A}}
which satisfies
(\phi\circ\psi)^{*}\theta_{\psi}\big|_{\phi(U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi})}=\theta_{\phi}\big|_{\phi(U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi})}
whenever
U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi}\neq\varnothing
.

We claim that
\big\{d\theta_{\phi}\big\}_{\phi\in\mathscr{A}}
defines a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}

(k+1)
-form on
\mathfrak{M}
(see Definition
4.2
). Namely, we claim

(\phi\circ\psi^{-1})^{*}d\theta_{\psi}\big|_{\phi(U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi})}=d\theta_{\phi}\big|_{\phi(U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi})},\quad\text{ whenever }\phi,\psi\in\mathscr{A}\text{ satisfy }U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi}\neq\varnothing,

(4.13)

and their common value on
\phi(U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi})
is
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

Indeed, once (
4.13
) is shown, then
\tau=\{d\theta_{\phi}\}_{\phi\in\mathscr{A}}
is the desired
(k+1)
-form. To see that this
\tau
is closed in the sense
of the statement of the lemma, note that if
F\in\mathscr{A}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-coordinate chart on
\mathfrak{M}
, then
F_{*}\tau=d\theta_{F}
and therefore

dF_{*}\tau=d^{2}\theta_{F}=0
.

First, we claim that
d\theta_{\phi}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
for every
\phi\in\mathscr{A}
.
The assumption that
d\theta
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})

(see Definition
2.9
) says that we can find a covering of coordinate charts
\{\phi_{j}:U_{\phi_{j}}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\}_{j\in I}\subseteq\mathscr{A}
(that is
\bigcup_{j}U_{\phi_{j}}=\mathfrak{M}
) such that
d(\phi_{j})_{*}\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U_{\phi_{j}};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}(U_{\phi_{j}})\right)
, i.e.
d\theta_{\phi_{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U_{\phi_{j}};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}(U_{\phi_{j}})\right)
for each
j\in I
.

Let
\psi\in\mathscr{A}
. For each
p\in U_{\psi}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
, we can find a
j_{0}\in I
such that
p\in U_{\phi_{j_{0}}}
. By Proposition
2.8
we see that
d(\psi\circ\phi_{j_{0}})^{*}\theta_{\phi_{j_{0}}}\big|_{\psi(U_{\psi}\cap U_{\phi_{j_{0}}})}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\psi(U_{\psi}\cap U_{\phi_{j_{0}}});{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
. By (
4.2
),
d\theta_{\psi}\big|_{\psi(U_{\psi}\cap U_{\phi_{j_{0}}})}=d(\psi\circ\phi_{j_{0}})^{*}\theta_{\phi_{j_{0}}}\big|_{\psi(U_{\psi}\cap U_{\phi_{j_{0}}})}
so
d\theta_{\psi}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
near
p\in U_{\psi}
. Since
p
is arbitrary, we know
d\theta_{\psi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\psi(U_{\psi});{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
. Therefore,
\{d\theta_{\phi}\}
is a collection of
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-forms.

We turn to proving (
4.13
).
Let
p\in U_{\phi}
,
and let
V\Subset U_{\phi}
be a neighborhood of
p
.
We will show:

(\phi\circ\psi^{-1})^{*}d\theta_{\psi}\big|_{\phi(V\cap U_{\psi})}=d\theta_{\phi}\big|_{\phi(V\cap U_{\psi})}\text{ whenever }\psi\in\mathscr{A}\text{ satisfies }V\cap U_{\psi}\neq\varnothing.

(4.14)

Since
\phi(V)\Subset\phi(U_{\phi})
, by Lemma
4.8
there exists a
\rho\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\phi(V);{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
and
\xi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\phi(V);{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k-1}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
such that
\theta_{\phi}\big|_{\phi(V)}=\rho+d\xi
, and
therefore

d\theta_{\phi}\big|_{\phi(V)}=d\rho
.

By Lemma
4.4

(iii)
, since
\beta>1-\alpha
and
\gamma+1>1-\alpha
,

d(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}\rho=(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}d\rho,\quad d(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}\xi=(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}d\xi,\qquad\text{on }\psi(V\cap U_{\psi}).

Therefore on
\psi(V\cap U_{\psi})
,

(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}d\theta_{\phi}=(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}d\rho=d(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}\rho=d(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}\theta_{\phi}-d(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}d\xi=d\theta_{\psi}-d^{2}(\psi\circ\phi^{-1})^{*}\xi=d\theta_{\psi}.

This proves (
4.14
).
Since
p\in U_{\phi}
was arbitrary,
(
4.13
) follows, completing the proof.
∎

In the proof of Theorem 3.1, we need a version of Proposition
2.8
on 1-forms where we keep track of various estimates.
We are concerned with the case when

F
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
and
is close to the identity map, and our 1-form
\theta
defined on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
is such that
\|\theta\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|d\theta\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}
is small.

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in[\alpha,\alpha+1]
be two real numbers, then there is a constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(n,\alpha,\beta)>1
satisfying the following:

Suppose
R\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies
R\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
and
\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\leq C_{0}^{-1}
, then the map
F:=\mathrm{id}+R:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{B}^{n}
. Moreover,

(i)

Let
\Phi=(\phi^{1},\dots,\phi^{n}):\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{B}^{n}
be the inverse map of
F
. Then,

\|g\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq C_{0}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})},\quad\forall g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n}).

(4.15)

In particular
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{0}
.

(ii)

\|\nabla\Phi-I_{n}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\leq C_{0}\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
.

(iii)

If
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}\left(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n}\right)
satisfies
\operatorname{supp}\theta\subsetneq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
and
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}\left(\mathbb{B}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n}\right)
, then
\operatorname{supp}F_{*}\theta\subsetneq\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}
and

\|d(F_{*}\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}\left(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n}\right)}\leq C_{0}\|d\theta\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}\left(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n}\right)}.

(4.16)

In the proof of Proposition
4.10
we need to follow convention for matrix-valued functions.

Convention 4.11
.

For matrix-valued map
A=(a_{i}^{j}):\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{M}^{n\times n}
, we use the matrix norm

|A(x)|_{\mathbb{M}^{n\times n}}:=\sup\limits_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}}\frac{|A(x)\cdot v|}{|v|},\quad\|A\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}=\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}|A(x)|_{\mathbb{M}^{n\times n}}.

(4.17)

For Zygmund-Hölder norms of
A
we use the component-wise norm; namely,
\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}:=\|(a_{1}^{1},\dots,a_{n}^{n})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}})}
.

Remark 4.12
.

Let
\alpha>0
, and let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set. It follows
from Lemma
2.2
that there is a
\tilde{C}_{U,\alpha}>0
, such that

\|AB\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\leq\tilde{C}_{U,\alpha}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})},\quad\forall A,B\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{M}^{n\times n}).

(4.18)

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
\alpha>0
, and let
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open ball. There is a
\tilde{c}_{B,\alpha}>0
, such that if
\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(B;\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}<\tilde{c}_{B,\alpha}
, then
I+A(x)
is an invertible matrix for every
x\in B
. In addition, the map

A\mapsto(I+A)^{-1}:\{M\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(B;\mathbb{M}^{n\times n}):\|M\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}<\tilde{c}_{B,\alpha}\}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(B;\mathbb{M}^{n\times n}),

is continuous and satisfies
\|(I+A)^{-1}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(B;\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\leq 2\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(B;\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}
.

Proof.

We take
\tilde{c}_{B,\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}\min\{\tilde{C}_{B,\alpha}^{-1},1/2\}<\frac{1}{2}
where
\tilde{C}_{B,\alpha}
is in (
4.18
).

When
\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}<\tilde{c}_{B,\alpha}
, we have
\|A^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\tilde{C}_{B,\alpha}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\|A^{k-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|A^{k-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Therefore, for such
A
we have
\|A^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq 2^{1-k}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq 2^{-k}
.

We know
(I+A)^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}A^{k}
whenever the right hand side absolutely converges. This power series has
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-norm convergent radius larger than
\tilde{c}_{B,\alpha}
and is continuous in the domain
\{\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}<\tilde{c}_{B,\alpha}\}
.

Finally we have

\|(I+A)^{-1}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}=\Big\|\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}A^{k}\Big\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\|A^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{1-k}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq 2\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}.

∎

Proof of Proposition
4.10
.

We let
C_{0}
be a large constant which may change from line to line.
In particular, we will choose
C_{0}
large enough such that
\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\leq C_{0}^{-1}
implies
\|R\|_{C^{0}}+\|\nabla R\|_{C^{0}}\leq\frac{1}{4}
and
\|\nabla R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\tilde{c}_{\mathbb{B}^{n},\alpha}
, where
\tilde{c}_{\mathbb{B}^{n},\alpha}
is in Lemma
4.13
.

By Lemma
4.13
,
\nabla F(x)=I+\nabla R(x)
is an invertible matrix for every
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
, and we have

\|(\nabla F)^{-1}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}=\|(I+\nabla R)^{-1}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq 2\|\nabla R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}.

(4.19)

Since
\|\nabla R\|_{C^{0}}\leq\frac{1}{4}
, we have
|R(x_{1})-R(x_{2})|\leq\|\nabla R\|_{C^{0}}|x_{1}-x_{2}|\leq\frac{1}{4}|x_{1}-x_{2}|
, which implies

|F(x_{1})-F(x_{2})|\geq|x_{1}-x_{2}|-|R(x_{1})-R(x_{2})|\geq\textstyle\frac{3}{4}|x_{1}-x_{2}|.

(4.20)

This implies
F
is injective. By the Inverse Function Theorem, we know
F:\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}F(\mathbb{B}^{n})
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism.

The assumption
R\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
gives
F(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})=\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}
. Since
F(\mathbb{B}^{n})
is contractible and
\overline{F(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\supset\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}
, we get that
F(\mathbb{B}^{n})=\mathbb{B}^{n}
. We conclude
F
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

(i)
:
First, we claim that there is a
C_{1}(n,\alpha,\beta)>0
, which does not
depend on
R
, such that whenever
R
satisfies the assumptions
of the proposition, we have

\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{1}.

(4.21)

Since
\Phi
is the inverse map of
F
, by
[
Str21
, Lemma 5.9]
, we know
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
only depends on
n
,
\alpha
,
\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
, and
\|(\nabla F)^{-1}\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}
.
We will show that
\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
and
\|(\nabla F)^{-1}\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}
have bounds that do not depend on
R
.

We have
\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\|\mathrm{id}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\|\mathrm{id}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\frac{1}{4}
. The right hand side of this inequality does not depend on
R
.

By (
4.20
),
|F(x_{1})-F(x_{2})|\geq\frac{3}{4}|x_{1}-x_{2}|
implies
\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}|(\nabla F(x))^{-1}|\leq\frac{4}{3}
, so
\|(\nabla F)^{-1}\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\leq\frac{4}{3}
, which does not depend on
R
as well. This establishes (
4.21
).

By
[
Str21
, Lemma 5.8]
, we know for every
\tilde{C}_{1}>0
there is a
C_{2}=C_{2}(\alpha,\beta,\tilde{C}_{1})>0
such that
\|g\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq C_{2}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
holds when
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\tilde{C}_{1}
. By possibly increasing
C_{0}
so that
C_{0}\geq C_{2}(\alpha,\beta,C_{1})
, we obtain
\|g\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq C_{0}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
, which is (
4.15
).

(ii)
: Note that the identity matrix
I
can be viewed as a constant function defined on the unit ball. Since
\Phi
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
, we get the equality
I=I\circ\Phi
as a matrix function on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

By the chain rule
I=\nabla(F\circ\Phi)=((\nabla F)\circ\Phi)\cdot\nabla\Phi
, so
\nabla\Phi-I=(\nabla F)^{-1}\circ\Phi-I=((\nabla F)^{-1}-I)\circ\Phi
. By (
4.19
)
and
(i)
, we have

\|\nabla\Phi-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}=\|((\nabla F)^{-1}-I)\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq C_{0}\|(\nabla F)^{-1}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq 2C_{0}\|\nabla R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})},

and we obtain
(ii)
by
replacing
C_{0}
with
2C_{0}
.

(iii)
: Let
\theta\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
be as in the assumption of
(iii)
. In particular,
\operatorname{supp}\theta\subsetneq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

By the assumption
\|R\|_{C^{0}}\leq\frac{1}{4}
, we have
F(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n})\subseteq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}+\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}=\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}
, so
\operatorname{supp}F_{*}\theta=F(\operatorname{supp}\theta)\subseteq\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

Similar to the proof of Lemma
4.8
, we define a 1-form
\rho
and a function
\xi
by (see (
4.6
))

\rho=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{i}dx^{i}:=\mathcal{G}\ast\vartheta d\theta,\quad\xi:=\mathcal{G}\ast\vartheta\theta.

\rho
and
\xi
are globally defined in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
because
\theta
is compactly supported in
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

By Lemma
4.6
, we have
\rho\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
,
\xi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and
\rho+d\xi=\mathcal{G}\ast(\vartheta d+d\vartheta)\theta=\theta
.
Moreover, by Lemma
4.7
with
\gamma=\beta-2
and the assumption
\operatorname{supp}\vartheta d\theta\subseteq\operatorname{supp}\theta\subseteq\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}\Subset\mathbb{B}^{n}
, we have

\|\rho\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\beta}\|\vartheta d\theta\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}}\lesssim_{\beta}\|d\theta\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}.

(4.22)

By Lemma
4.4

(iii)
,
d(F_{*}d\xi)=d^{2}F_{*}\xi=0
on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
, so we have that

d(F_{*}\theta)=d\left(F_{*}\rho\big|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)+d\left(F_{*}d\xi\big|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)=d\left(F_{*}\rho\big|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\right)=d\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\rho_{i}\circ\Phi)d\phi^{i}\right),\quad\text{on }\mathbb{B}^{n}.

(4.23)

Thus, to prove (
4.16
), by (
4.22
) it suffices to show

\|d(F_{*}\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\left(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n}\right)}.

(4.24)

Fix
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
such that
\chi|_{\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}}\equiv 1
. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, set

\tilde{\rho}_{i}:=\chi(\rho_{i}\circ\Phi),\quad\tilde{\phi}^{i}:=\chi\phi^{i}.

So
\tilde{\rho}_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{c}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
and
d\tilde{\phi}^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}\left(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n}\right)
are globally defined 1-forms for each
i
, such that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tilde{\rho}_{i}d\tilde{\phi}^{i})\big|_{\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left((\rho_{i}\circ\Phi)d\phi^{i}\right)\big|_{\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=(F_{*}\rho)\big|_{\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

(4.25)

By (
4.25
) and (
4.23
) we have
