be an arbitrary precompact open subset of
U
. We fix
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
such that
\chi\big|_{V}\equiv 1
so
\chi f
is defined in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Since we have
\partial_{x^{j}}f\big|_{V}=\partial_{x^{j}}(\chi f)\big|_{V}
,
j=1,\dots,n
, it is enough to prove that
\tilde{f}:=\chi f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
if and only if
\tilde{f},\partial_{x^{1}}\tilde{f},\dots,\partial_{x^{n}}\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Clearly
\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
implies
\partial_{x^{1}}\tilde{f},\dots,\partial_{x^{n}}\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Conversely, suppose
\tilde{f},\partial_{x^{1}}\tilde{f},\dots,\partial_{x^{n}}\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
hold, we have
(I+\bigtriangleup)\tilde{f}=\tilde{f}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{x^{j}}(\partial_{x^{j}}\tilde{f})\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Since
I+\bigtriangleup:\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a isomorphism of Banach spaces for every
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
(see
[
Tri10
, Theorem 2.3.8]
), we have
\tilde{f}=(I+\bigtriangleup)^{-1}(I+\bigtriangleup)\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, completing the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.2

(i)
,
(ii)
, and
(iii)
.

By passing to a local coordinate chart it suffices to prove the results on an open subset
\mathfrak{M}=U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
endowed with the standard coordinate system
(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
. In this coordinate system we write

X_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{i}^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}},\quad\text{with }a_{i}^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U).

(6.1)

The assumption that
X_{1},\dots,X_{q}
span the tangent space at every point shows that the matrix function
(a_{i}^{j})_{n\times q}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{M}^{n\times q})
has full rank
n
at every point. So we can find a matrix
(b_{j}^{i})_{q\times n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{M}^{q\times n})
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{q}b_{j}^{i}a_{i}^{k}=\delta_{j}^{k}=\begin{cases}1,&j=k,\\
0,&j\neq k.\end{cases}

(6.2)

(i)
: By Definition
2.13
,
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
holds for
\beta\in(-\alpha,1]
. For
\beta\in(0,\alpha+1]
we proceed by induction on
r=\lceil\beta\rceil
. The base case
r=1
, which is
\beta\in(0,1]
, follows from the definition. Let
r\geq 2
and suppose we have the case
\lceil\beta\rceil=r-1
, i.e.
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
for all
\beta\in(r-1,r]
, we wish to prove it for
\beta\in(r,\min(r+1,\alpha+1)]
.

Let
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
, so
f\in C^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
and by Proposition
2.7

(iv)
,
X_{1}f,\dots,X_{q}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. By the inductive hypothesis
f\in C^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
and
X_{1}f,\dots,X_{q}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
, so
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
.

Conversely, suppose
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
, so
f\in C^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
and
X_{1}f,\dots,X_{q}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. By the inductive hypothesis
f,X_{1}f,\dots,X_{q}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. Using (
6.2
) and Lemma
2.2
we have
\partial_{j}f=\sum_{i=1}^{q}\sum_{k=1}^{n}b_{j}^{i}a_{i}^{k}\partial_{k}f=\sum_{i=1}^{q}b_{j}^{i}X_{i}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
for
j=1,\dots,n
. By Lemma
6.3
we obtain
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
.

(ii)
: Here
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
and Definition
2.14
imply that
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
holds for
\beta\in(-\alpha,\frac{1}{2}]
.

For
\beta\in(-\frac{1}{2},\alpha]
we prove
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)

by induction on
r=\lceil\beta+\frac{1}{2}\rceil
. The base case
r=1
. which is
\beta\in(-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}]
, was established above. Let
r\geq 2
and suppose we have the case
\lceil\beta+\frac{1}{2}\rceil=r-1
, i.e.
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
for all
\beta\in(r-\frac{3}{2},r-\frac{1}{2}]
, we wish to prove it for
\beta\in(r-\frac{1}{2},\min(r+\frac{1}{2},\alpha)]
.

Suppose
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
, so
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
and by Proposition
2.7

(v)
,
[X_{1},Y],\dots,[X_{q},Y]\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
. By the inductive hypothesis
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
and
[X_{1},Y],\dots,[X_{q},Y]\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
, which is the definition of
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
(see Definition
2.14
).

Conversely, suppose
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
, so
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
and
[X_{1},Y],\dots,[X_{q},Y]\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
. By the inductive hypothesis
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\max(\frac{1}{2},\beta-1)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
and
[X_{1},Y],\dots,[X_{q},Y]\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
.

Write
Y=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\rho^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}
where
\rho^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\frac{1}{2},\beta-1)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. By Lemma
2.2
we know the equation below is defined.

[X_{i},Y]=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\left(a_{i}^{j}\frac{\partial\rho^{k}}{\partial x^{j}}-\rho^{j}\frac{\partial a_{i}^{k}}{\partial x^{j}}\right)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(X_{i}\rho^{k})\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}+L_{i}(Y),\quad 1\leq i\leq q.

(6.3)

Here
L_{1},\dots,L_{q}
are multiplication operators (0-th order differential operators) with
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-coefficients.

By Lemma
2.2
, since
\max(\frac{1}{2},\beta-1)>1-\alpha
, we have
L_{i}(Y)\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
, so
\rho^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
and
X_{i}\rho^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
for
1\leq i\leq q
,
1\leq k\leq n
. Using (
6.2
) and Lemma
2.2
we have
\partial_{j}\rho^{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{q}b_{j}^{i}\cdot(X_{i}\rho^{k})\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
, for
j=1,\dots,n
. By Lemma
6.3
we obtain
\rho^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
for all
1\leq k\leq n
, which means
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
. This finishes the induction argument and hence the proof of
(ii)
.

The proof of
(iii)
is the same as
(ii)
. Indeed, similar to (
6.3
), we can write

\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}\theta=\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<\dots<j_{k}\leq n}^{n}(X_{i}\theta_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}})dx^{j_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{j_{k}}+L_{i}^{\prime}(\theta),\quad 1\leq i\leq q.

where
L_{1}^{\prime},\dots,L_{n}^{\prime}
are product operators on
k
-forms with
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-coefficients. Using the same method as for
(ii)
we can prove that
\theta_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
if and only if
\theta_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}},X_{1}\theta_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}},\dots,X_{n}\theta_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
if and only if
\theta_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. We omit the details.
∎

Corollary 6.4
.

Let
\alpha\geq 1
and let
X_{1},\dots,X_{q}
be
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-vector fields on a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifold
\mathfrak{M}
that span the tangent space at every point. Let
\beta>1-\alpha
and
1\leq k\leq n
.

(i)

Let
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{(-\alpha)^{+}}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
be a
k
-form. Then
d\theta
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
if and only if
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
.

(ii)

Let
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
, then
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
.

Proof.

By passing to a local coordinate chart it suffices to prove the results on an open subset
\mathfrak{M}=U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

(i)
: First we prove the case
\beta\in(1-\alpha,1]
. By Definition,
2.16
,
d\theta
having regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
is equivalent as
d\theta
having regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
when
\beta\in(-\alpha,1]
. By Lemma
2.10
,
d\theta
having regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
is equivalent as
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
for
\beta\in(1-\alpha,\alpha+1]
. By Proposition
6.2

(iii)
,
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
. So
d\theta
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
if and only if
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
.

For
\beta\in(0,\infty)
we proceed by induction on
r=\lceil\beta\rceil
. The base case
r=1
, which is the case
\beta\in(0,1]\subseteq(1-\alpha,1]
, was established above. Let
r\geq 2
and suppose we have the case
\lceil\beta\rceil=r-1
, i.e.
(i)
holds for all
\beta\in(r-1,r]
and we wish to prove it for
\beta\in(r,r+1]
.

Suppose
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
. By Definition
2.15
,
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{0^{+}}_{\mathrm{loc}}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}
and
d\iota_{X_{i}}d\theta=\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
for all
i=1,\dots,q
. By the inductive hypothesis
d\theta,d\iota_{X_{1}}d\theta,\dots,d\iota_{X_{q}}d\theta
have regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
, which is
the definition of

d\theta
having regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
by Definition
2.15
.

Conversely, suppose
d\theta
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
, i.e.
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{0^{+}}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
and
d\theta,d\iota_{X_{1}}d\theta,\dots,d\iota_{X_{q}}d\theta
have regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. Applying the inductive hypothesis to
\theta,\iota_{X_{1}}d\theta,\dots,\iota_{X_{q}}d\theta
we have
d\theta,d\iota_{X_{1}}d\theta,\dots,d\iota_{X_{q}}d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
. Note that
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{0^{+}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
implies
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}
for some
\varepsilon>0
, and by assumption
X_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, so by Proposition
2.7

(vi)
,
\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}d\theta
is defined and
\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}d\theta=d\iota_{X_{i}}d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
. By Definition
2.15
we get
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
, finishing the induction argument.

(ii)
: For the case
\beta\in(1-\alpha,1]
, by Proposition
6.2

(iii)
, since
\alpha>1
, we have
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U)
and
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
. Therefore
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}
implies
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}
.

For
\beta\in(0,\infty)
we proceed the induction on
r=\lceil\beta\rceil
. The base case
r=1
, i.e.
\beta\in(0,1]
, was established above. Let
r\geq 2
and suppose we have the case
\lceil\beta\rceil=r-1
, i.e.
\beta\in(r-1,r]
, we wish to prove the case
\beta\in(r,r+1]
.

Let
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
, so by assumption
\theta,\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{1}}\theta,\dots,\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{q}}\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k}T^{*}U\right)
. By the inductive hypothesis
d\theta,d\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{1}}\theta,\dots,d\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{q}}\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
. Since
\beta>1
and
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{0^{+}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, by Proposition
2.7

(vi)
,
\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}d\theta
is defined, and therefore
\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}d\theta=d\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}
. So by Proposition
6.2

(iii)
if
\beta\leq\frac{3}{2}
, or by Definition
2.15
if
\beta\geq\frac{3}{2}
, we get
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.2

(iv)
.

By passing to a local coordinate chart it suffices to prove the result on an open subset
\mathfrak{M}=U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

By Corollary
6.4

(i)
,
d\theta
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
if and only if
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
. By Proposition
6.2

(iii)
, since
\beta-1\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
,
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
. So
d\theta
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
if and only if
d\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{k+1}T^{*}U\right)
.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

Proposition
6.2

(iv)
and Corollary
6.4

(i)
and
(ii)
also hold for general
\alpha>0
. However, the proof is more complicated and is not used in the proof of our main results. Because of this,
we do not include these more general results.

7
The Proof of the Main Theorem

In this section, we prove Theorem
3.1
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma>\frac{1}{2}
. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{q}
be
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-vector fields on a manifold
\mathfrak{M}
that span the tangent space at every point, and let
\Phi:\mathfrak{N}\to\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1}
-diffeomorphism.

Let
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
,
Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
and
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
, then
f\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
,
\langle\theta,Y\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
, and
\langle\Phi^{*}\theta,\Phi^{*}Y\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\Phi^{*}X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{N})
.

Proof.

First we prove
\langle\theta,Y\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
. The argument to show
f\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
is similar and we omit the details.

Consider first
\beta\in(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}]
. By assumption (see Definitions
2.14
and
2.15
) we see that
Y,\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and
\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{1}}Y,\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{1}}\theta,\dots,\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{q}}Y,\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{q}}\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
. So by Proposition
2.7

(i)
,
\langle\theta,Y\rangle=\iota_{Y}\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
satisfies

X_{i}\langle\theta,Y\rangle=\langle\theta,\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}Y\rangle+\langle\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}\theta,Y\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M}),\quad\text{for all }i=1,\dots,q.

By Proposition
6.2

(i)
, since
\beta\leq\alpha+1
, we get
\langle\theta,Y\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
.

For
\beta\in(\frac{1}{2},\infty)
, we proceed by induction on
r=\lceil\beta+\frac{1}{2}\rceil
. The base case,
r=2
, is established above. Let
r\geq 3
and suppose the result is true for
\lceil\beta+\frac{1}{2}\rceil=r-1
, i.e. for
\beta\in(r-\frac{3}{2},r-\frac{1}{2}]
, we wish to prove it for
\beta\in(r-\frac{1}{2},r+\frac{1}{2}]
.

By assumption
Y,\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}
and
Y,\theta,\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{1}}Y,\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{1}}\theta,\dots,\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{q}}Y,\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{q}}\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}
. By the inductive hypothesis
\langle\theta,Y\rangle,\langle\theta,\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}Y\rangle,\langle\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}\theta,Y\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
for all
i=1,\dots,q
. So
X_{i}\langle\theta,Y\rangle=\langle\theta,\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}Y\rangle+\langle\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}\theta,Y\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
for all
i=1,\dots,q
as well. By Definition
2.13
we get
\langle\theta,Y\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
. This completes the induction argument.

Finally, we show
\langle\Phi^{*}\theta,\Phi^{*}Y\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\Phi^{*}X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{N})
. Note that
\langle\theta,Y\rangle=\iota_{Y}\theta
. So by Lemma
4.4

(i)
and Remark
6.1

(i)
we get
\langle\Phi^{*}\theta,\Phi^{*}Y\rangle=\iota_{\Phi^{*}Y}\Phi^{*}\theta=\Phi^{*}(\iota_{Y}\theta)=\Phi^{*}\langle\theta,Y\rangle
and their common value is in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\Phi^{*}X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{N})
.
∎

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
and
\beta\in[\alpha,\alpha+1]
. Assume that
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}
form a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis for the cotangent bundle of a manifold
\mathfrak{M}
. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be its dual basis.

Suppose
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
then
d\lambda^{1},\dots,d\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
.

Proof.

The inverse of a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-matrix is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-matrix, so as the dual basis of
(\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n})
, we know
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-vector fields on
\mathfrak{M}
.

Write
c_{ij}^{k}:=\langle\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}\lambda^{k},X_{j}\rangle
for
1\leq i,j,k\leq n
. Since
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
and
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}
are dual bases, we have

d\lambda^{k}(X_{i},X_{j})=X_{i}\langle\lambda^{k},X_{j}\rangle-\langle\lambda^{k},[X_{i},X_{j}]\rangle-X_{j}\langle\lambda^{k},X_{i}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}\lambda^{k},X_{j}\rangle-0=c_{ij}^{k}\ \Rightarrow\ d\lambda^{k}=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}c_{ij}^{k}\lambda^{i}\wedge\lambda^{j}.

(7.1)

Note that the products in (
7.1
) are all defined due to Lemma
2.2
.

By Definition
2.15
,
\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}\lambda^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
. By Proposition
6.2

(iii)
, since
\beta-1\leq\alpha
, we have
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T^{*}\mathfrak{M})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
, so
\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}\lambda^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
.
Since
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, by Proposition
2.7

(i)
,
\langle\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{i}}\lambda^{k},X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
; i.e.
c_{ij}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
. By Proposition
2.7

(ii)
, since
\beta-1+\alpha>0
, we know
\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}c_{ij}^{k}\lambda^{i}\wedge\lambda^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
, so by (
7.1
), we conclude that
d\lambda^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathfrak{M};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\right)
for
1\leq k\leq n
.
∎

Remark 7.3
.

Lemma
7.2
is similar to Corollary
6.4

(ii)
. However, it does not follow from Corollary
6.4

(ii)
as we require
\alpha\geq 1
in Corollary
6.4

(ii)
.

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

The case
\beta\leq\alpha
, for each condition is trivial.
Indeed, fix
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, take any
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-coordinate chart
\Phi^{-1}:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{B}^{n}
near
p
. We have
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})
and
\Phi^{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
. By shrinking the domain and scaling, we can make replace
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
by
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
. In other words, we have
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})
and
\Phi^{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
. Therefore,
(a)
and
(b)
are automatically satisfied for
\alpha>0
, and the same for
3
and
4
when
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
.
For the remainder of the proof, we assume
\beta>\alpha
.

We will show
(a)

\Leftrightarrow

(b)
for arbitrary
\alpha>0
, and
(a)

\Rightarrow

4

\Rightarrow

3

\Rightarrow

(b)
when
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
.

We first prove that
(a)
\Rightarrow
(b)
for arbitrary
\alpha>0
.

Suppose
(a)
holds, and thus there exists a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
and a diffeomorphism
\Phi:\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
as in
(a)
. Since by assumption
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})
and the inverse of
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-matrix is still
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-matrix, we see that the dual basis
\Phi^{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
. So
d\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and
\langle\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i},\Phi^{*}X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq j\leq n
.

By Proposition
6.2

(i)
, since
\beta<\beta+1
, we have
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\Phi^{*}X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
. Thus,
\langle\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i},\Phi^{*}X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\Phi^{*}X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq j\leq q
.

By Definition
2.11
,
d\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
is the same as
d\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i}
having regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
. By Proposition
6.2

(iv)
, if
\beta\geq 1
(where we use
\alpha=\beta
and
\Phi^{*}X\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
in the proposition) or by Definition
2.16
if
\beta\leq 1
(where
\beta-1\leq 0
),
d\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i}
having regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
is the same as
d\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i}
having regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\Phi^{*}X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
.

Now
d\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i}
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\Phi^{*}X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
and
\langle\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i},\Phi^{*}X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\Phi^{*}X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq j\leq q
. Note that
\Phi_{*}=(\Phi^{-1})^{*}
. By Remark
6.1

(iv)
,
d\lambda^{i}=d\Phi_{*}\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i}
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\Phi_{*}\Phi^{*}X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
for
1\leq i\leq n
. And by Lemma
7.1
,
\langle\lambda^{i},X_{j}\rangle=\langle\Phi_{*}\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i},\Phi_{*}\Phi^{*}X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\Phi_{*}\Phi^{*}X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
, finishing the direction
(a)

\Rightarrow

(b)
.

Next we prove
(a)
\Rightarrow
4
by assuming
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
.

By assumption of
(a)

\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})
. By Proposition
6.2

(ii)
,
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\Phi^{*}X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})
, so
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\Phi^{*}X,\mathrm{loc}}
. By Remark
6.1

(ii)
, where we use
\Phi_{*}=(\Phi^{-1})^{*}
, we have
X_{j}=\Phi_{*}\Phi^{*}X_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\Phi_{*}\Phi^{*}X}(U;TU)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X}(U;TU)
for
1\leq j\leq q
. Therefore, we get
(a)

\Rightarrow

4
.

We now prove
(b)
\Rightarrow
(a)

for all
\alpha>0
. Fix
p\in\mathfrak{M}
. We proceed by induction on
r=\lceil\beta\rceil
.

We start with the base case
r=1
, i.e.
\beta\in(0,1]
. And as mentioned in the beginning of the proof we assume
\beta>\alpha

and therefore
\alpha<1
. By Definition
2.16
,
(b)
is equivalent to
d\lambda^{1},\dots,d\lambda^{n}
having regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
. By Corollary
5.17
there is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism
\Phi:\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
such that
\Phi(0)=p
and
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})
.

We let
X^{0}
denote the sub-collection of
X
given by

X^{0}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n}).

So
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X^{0},\mathrm{loc}}(U)
.
By assumption
\langle\lambda^{i},X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X^{0},\mathrm{loc}}(U)
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq q
. Applying Lemma
7.1
to
\langle\lambda^{i},X_{j}\rangle
with
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{q})
in that lemma replaced by
X^{0}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
, we get
\langle\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i},\Phi^{*}X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\Phi^{*}X^{0},\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
. By Definition
2.13
, we have
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\Phi^{*}X^{0},\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
since
\beta\leq 1
. Therefore
\langle\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i},\Phi^{*}X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
and thus

\Phi^{*}X_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\langle\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i},\Phi^{*}X_{j}\rangle\Phi^{*}X_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n}),\quad n+1\leq j\leq q.

(7.2)

Now let
r\geq 1
and suppose
(b)

\Rightarrow

(a)
is true for the case
\lceil\beta\rceil\leq r
, i.e. for
\beta\in(0,r]
, we wish to prove the case
\beta\in(r,r+1]
.

By the inductive hypothesis, there is a neighborhood
U_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
and a
\mathscr{C}^{r+1}
-diffeomorphism
\Phi_{0}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}U_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
such that
\Phi_{0}(0)=p
and
\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{r}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})
. Since the inverse of
\mathscr{C}^{r}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-matrix is still a
\mathscr{C}^{r}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-matrix and
\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{r}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, for the dual basis we have
\Phi_{0}^{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,\Phi_{0}^{*}\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{r}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
.

By assumption,
d\lambda^{i}
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U_{0})
, so by Remark
6.1

(iv)
,
d\Phi_{0}^{*}\lambda^{i}
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\Phi_{0}^{*}X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
for
i=1,\dots,n
. By Proposition
6.2

(iv)
, since
\Phi_{0}^{*}X\in\mathscr{C}^{r}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and
\beta\leq r+1
, we know that
d\Phi_{0}^{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,d\Phi_{0}^{*}\lambda^{n}
have regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
.
By Definition
2.11
, this is to say
d\Phi_{0}^{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,d\Phi_{0}^{*}\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathbb{B}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n}\right)
.

Applying Corollary
5.17

2

\Rightarrow

1

to
d\Phi_{0}^{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,d\Phi_{0}^{*}\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(\mathbb{B}^{n};{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n}\right)
, since by assumption
\beta-1\in(r-1,r]
, we can find a map
\Phi_{1}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{B}^{n}
that is
\mathscr{C}^{r+1}
-diffeomorphism onto its image, such that
\Phi_{1}(0)=0
and
\Phi_{1}^{*}\Phi_{0}^{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,\Phi_{1}^{*}\Phi_{0}^{*}\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
. Since the inverse of
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-matrix is still a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-matrix, see that the dual basis
\Phi_{1}^{*}\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi_{1}^{*}\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})
.

Take
\Phi=\Phi_{0}\circ\Phi_{1}
and
U=\Phi(\mathbb{B}^{n})\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
. So
\Phi(0)=\Phi_{0}(\Phi_{1}(0))=\Phi_{0}(0)=p
and
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})
. We are going to prove
\Phi^{*}X_{n+1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})
.

We still use
X^{0}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
as the sub-collection of
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{q})
. By assumption
(b)
,
\langle\lambda^{i},X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X^{0},\mathrm{loc}}(U)
, so applying Lemma
7.1
to
\Phi
and
X^{0}
, we have
\langle\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i},\Phi^{*}X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\Phi^{*}X^{0},\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
for all
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq j\leq q
. By Proposition
6.2

(i)
, since
\Phi^{*}X^{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, we have
\langle\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i},\Phi^{*}X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
for all
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq j\leq q
. Therefore using (
7.2
) we get
\Phi^{*}X_{n+1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})
.

We conclude
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T\mathbb{B}^{n})
. Replacing
\Phi(t)
by
\Phi(\frac{1}{2}t)
for
t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
, we can replace
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
by
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
, finishing the induction argument and hence the proof of
(b)

\Rightarrow

(a)
.

Now assume
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
, we will show
4

\Rightarrow

3

\Rightarrow

(b)
. As mentioned in the beginning of the proof we assume
\beta>\alpha
. In particular, we assume
\beta>\frac{1}{2}
.

In the following proof, we fix a neighborhood
U
of
p\in\mathfrak{M}
where
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
form a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis on
U
.

4
\Rightarrow
3
: By assumption
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
, by Definition
2.14
since
\beta>\frac{1}{2}
, we have
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
. The inverse of
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-matrix is still a
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-matrix, so for the dual basis of
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
we have
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
.
To prove
3
, by Definition
2.15
, it remains to show
\lambda^{i},\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}\lambda^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq q
.

For
1\leq i,j\leq n
,
\langle\lambda^{i},X_{j}\rangle=\delta_{j}^{i}
is a constant function, so
0=\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}\langle\lambda^{i},X_{j}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}\lambda^{i},X_{j}\rangle+\langle\lambda^{i},\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}X_{j}\rangle
. Therefore

\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}\lambda^{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\langle\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}\lambda^{i},X_{j}\rangle\lambda^{j}=-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\langle\lambda^{i},\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}X_{j}\rangle\lambda^{j},\quad\text{for }1\leq i\leq n,1\leq k\leq q.

(7.3)

We prove
4
\Rightarrow
3
by induction on
r=\lceil\beta+\frac{1}{2}\rceil
, we work on the range
\beta\in(\frac{1}{2},\infty)
.

We start with the base case
r=2
which is the case
\beta\in(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}]
. By assumption
4
and Definition
2.14
, since
\beta-1\leq\frac{1}{2}
, we have
\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}X_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
for
1\leq j,k\leq q
. Since
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
, by Proposition
2.7

(i)
and
(ii)
we know
\langle\lambda^{i},\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and
\langle\lambda^{i},\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}X_{j}\rangle\lambda^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
for
1\leq i,j\leq n
,
1\leq k\leq q
. Using (
7.3
) we get
\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}\lambda^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq k\leq q
.

We have
\lambda^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
and
\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}\lambda^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq k\leq q
, so by Definition
2.15
,
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
. Note that by assumption
4
,
X_{1},\dots,X_{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
, so by Lemma
7.1
,
\langle\lambda^{i},X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
for
1\leq i\leq n,1\leq j\leq q
.

Now let
r\geq 3
and suppose
4

\Rightarrow

3
holds for the case
\lceil\beta+\frac{1}{2}\rceil=r-1
i.e. for
\beta\in(r-\frac{3}{2},r-\frac{1}{2}]
, we wish to prove it for
\beta\in(r-\frac{1}{2},r+\frac{1}{2}]
.

By Definition
2.14

X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
implies
\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}X_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
for
1\leq j,k\leq q
. By the inductive hypothesis we have
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
. So by Lemma
7.1
we get
\langle\lambda^{i},\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
and
\langle\lambda^{i},\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}X_{j}\rangle\lambda^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
. So by (
7.3
) we get
\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}\lambda^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq k\leq q
.

We have
\lambda^{i},\mathrm{Lie}_{X_{k}}\lambda^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq k\leq q
. By Definition
2.15
we get
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
. Since
X_{1},\dots,X_{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
, by Lemma
7.1
again we get
\langle\lambda^{i},X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq j\leq q
, finishing the induction argument and hence the proof of
4

\Rightarrow

3
.

3
\Rightarrow
(b)
: The result
\langle\lambda^{i},X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
follows from the assumption. We need is to show
d\lambda^{i}
has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
, for
1\leq i\leq n
.

First we assume
\alpha\geq 1
. By assumption
3
,
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
, so by Corollary
6.4

(ii)
we get
d\lambda^{1},\dots,d\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}^{\beta-1}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}T^{*}U\right)
.

We next consider
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
. First we assume
\beta\leq 1
. Note that we only need to consider
\beta\in(\alpha,1]
otherwise it is trivial.

We use
X^{0}=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
as the sub-collection of
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{q})
.
By assumption of the theorem,
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;TU)
. Since the inverse of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-matrix is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-matrix, for the dual basis of
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
we have
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
.

Note that
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X^{0},\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
. Since
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
and
(\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n})
are dual bases, applying Lemma
7.2
and using
that
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X^{0},\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}U)
, we obtain
d\lambda^{1},\dots,d\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\left(U;{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{2}T^{*}U\right)
. By Definition
2.11
this is the same as
d\lambda^{1},\dots,d\lambda^{n}
having regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. By Definition
2.16
, since
\beta-1\leq 0
, this is the equivalent to
d\lambda^{1},\dots,d\lambda^{n}
having regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
.

When
\beta>1
, by the established case
\beta=1
from above we know that
d\lambda^{1},\dots,d\lambda^{n}
have regularity
\mathscr{C}^{0}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. By assumption
3
,

\langle\lambda^{i},X_{j}\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
,
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq j\leq q
. Therefore, by the already proved implication
(b)

\Rightarrow

(a)
we can find a
\mathscr{C}^{2}
-atlas on
U
such that
X_{1}\big|_{U},\dots,X_{q}\big|_{U}
are
\mathscr{C}^{1}
on this atlas. That is to say we can assume
\alpha=1
in this case.
Since we have already established the case
\alpha\geq 1
, we see that
d\lambda^{1},\dots,d\lambda^{n}
have regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(U)
, completing the proof.
∎

8
Harmonic Coordinates and Canonical Coordinates

Given a non-smooth Riemannian metric
g
on a manifold,
\mathfrak{M}
,
DeTurck and Kazdan showed that
g
has optimal regularity
in harmonic coordinates
[
DK81
, Lemma 1.2]
(in
the Zygmund-Hölder sense),
but may not have optimal regularity in geodesic normal
coordinates
[
DK81
, Example 2.3]
(in fact, the regularity
of
g
in geodesic normal coordinates may be two derivatives worse than
the regularity in harmonic coordinates).

In this section, we present analogous results for vector fields.
Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be
C^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-vector fields on a
C^{2}
-manifold
\mathfrak{M}
that form a local basis for the tangent space at every point. In Section
5
we defined a Riemannian metric
g=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda^{i}\cdot\lambda^{i}
where
(\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n})
is the dual basis of
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
(see Remark
5.2
).
With respect to this metric,
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
form an orthonormal basis at every point. Since
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in C^{1}
,
we can talk about the metric Laplacian
\bigtriangleup_{g}
with respect to
g
.

Proposition 8.1
.

In harmonic coordinates with respect to
g
,
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
have optimal regularity.

More precisely, let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
and
g
be as above, and let
\beta>1
. Suppose there is a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}
-atlas
\mathscr{A}
which is compatible with the
C^{2}
-atlas of
\mathfrak{M}
, such that
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
on
\mathscr{A}
, let
\psi:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a harmonic coordinate chart
6
6

6

Such a harmonic coordinate chart
\psi
always exists locally when
\beta>1
, see also
[
DK81
, Lemma 1.2]
.
, then
\psi_{*}X_{1},\dots,\psi_{*}X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\psi
is a
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\beta+1}
-map with respect to
(\mathfrak{M},\mathscr{A})
. Once this is done, applying Lemma
4.1

(ii)
on
\Phi=\psi^{-1}
and using that
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
with respect to
(\mathfrak{M},\mathscr{A})
, we get that
\psi_{*}X_{1},\dots,\psi_{*}X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

Since the statement
\psi\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\beta+1}(U;V)
is local,
we may without loss of generality, shrink
U
.
By doing so, the hypotheses of the proposition
imply that there is a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}
-coordinate chart
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n}):U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
on
U
(respect to
\mathscr{A}
). In this coordinate chart we can write
\bigtriangleup_{g}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det g}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}(\sqrt{\det g}g^{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}})
where
g^{ij}
and
\sqrt{\det g}
are as in (
5.7
).

By assumption
\bigtriangleup_{g}\psi^{k}=0
for
k=1,\dots,n
. Note that on
(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
,
\bigtriangleup_{g}
is a second order divergent form elliptic operator with
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-coefficients. By a classical elliptic estimates (for example,
[
Tay11
, Proposition 4.1]
) we have that
\psi^{k}
are all
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
with respect to
\mathscr{A}
, completing the proof.
∎

Remark 8.2
.

In fact the coordinate chart we construct in Proposition
5.10
(also see (
5.8
)) is closely related to harmonic coordinates.

Remark 8.3
.

While Proposition
8.1
shows that
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
have optimal regularity with respect to harmonic coordinates, this fact along does not give a practical test for what the optimal regularity is. Theorem
3.1
, on the other hand, provides such a test.

Remark 8.4
.

Proposition
8.1
shows that harmonic coordinates induces a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}
-atlas with respect to which
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
. It is possible that the harmonic coordinates induces some
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1}
-atlas for some
\gamma>\beta
while
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are only
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
with respect to this atlas; see Example
8.5
.

Example 8.5
.

Endow
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with standard coordinates
(x,y)
, and let
\theta\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be a function which is not smooth. Set
X:=\cos(\theta(x,y))\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+\sin(\theta(x,y))\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
and
Y:=-\sin(\theta(x,y))\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+\cos(\theta(x,y))\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
. The corresponding metric is
g=dx^{2}+dy^{2}
since
X,Y
form an orthonormal basis with respect to the standard Euclidean metric, thus
\bigtriangleup_{g}=\bigtriangleup=-\partial_{x}^{2}-\partial_{y}^{2}
.

Therefore the singleton
\{(x,y):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}\}
is an atlas of harmonic coordinates for
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and since harmonic functions are real-analytic, we know the collection of harmonic coordinates with respect to
\bigtriangleup_{g}
defines an real-analytic structure for
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(which coincides with the standard real-analytic structure). Even though the differential structure induced by the harmonic coordinates is real analytic,
X
and
Y
cannot be smooth under any coordinate system (since they are not smooth with respect to these
harmonic coordinates).

As mentioned before,
DeTurck and Kazdan showed that a Riemannian metric may not have optimal
regularity with respect to
geodesic normal
coordinates
[
DK81
, Example 2.3]
.
A natural analog of geodesic normal coordiantes for vector fields
are canonical coordinates (of the first kind).
Next, we show that vector fields may not have optimal regularity
with respect to these canonical coordinates.

Given
C^{1}
-vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
on
\mathfrak{M}
that form a basis on the tangent space at every point, the canonical coordinates at
p\in\mathfrak{M}
is the map
\Phi_{p}(t^{1},\dots,t^{n}):=e^{t^{1}X_{1}+\dots+t^{n}X_{n}}p
defined via solving the ordinary differential equation, provided that it is solvable:

e^{t\cdot X}(p)=E(1),\quad{\text{where }}E:[0,1]\to\mathfrak{M},\quad\frac{d}{dr}E(r)=r\left(t^{1}X_{1}(E(r))+\dots+t^{n}X_{n}(E(r))\right),\quad E(0)=p.

(8.1)

When
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha>1
, classical regularity theorems for ODEs show that
\Phi_{p}
is at least
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
.
Therefore,
\Phi_{p}^{*}X_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{p}^{*}X_{n}
are at least
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
; which is one derivative less than the original
regularity of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
. The next result
shows that this loss of one derivative is sometimes inevitable.

Lemma 8.6
.

Endow
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with standard coordinate system
(x,y)
. Let
\alpha>1
and let
X:=\partial_{x}
and
Y:=xf(y)\partial_{x}+\partial_{y}
where
f(y):=\alpha\max(0,y)^{\alpha-1}
.

Then we can find a new
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
atlas
\mathscr{A}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
which is compatible with the standard
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, such that
X,Y
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
with respect to this the new atlas, but for the canonical coordinates
\Phi(t,s):=e^{tX+sY}(0)
we have
\Phi^{*}Y\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1+\varepsilon}
near
(0,0)
, in particular the collection
(\Phi^{*}X,\Phi^{*}Y)
is not
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1+\varepsilon}
.

Note that
X
and
Y
form a local basis of the tangent space at every point, and
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

First we show the existence of the new atlas
\mathscr{A}
with respect to which
X
and
Y
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
. In particular
X,Y
are
C^{1}
in
\mathscr{A}
, so (
8.1
) is uniquely solvable and hence
\Phi
is well-defined.

Note that
[X,Y]=f(y)\partial_{x}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Specifically, the dual basis of
X,Y
are 1-forms
\lambda=dx-xf(y)dy,\eta=dy
which satisfy that
d\lambda=f(y)dx\wedge dy
and
d\eta=0
are both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
2-forms, so the condition
(b)
in Theorem
3.1
is satisfied. By Theorem
3.1
we can find a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-atlas
\mathscr{A}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
X,Y
are both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
on
\mathscr{A}
.

Since
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}

are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\subsetneq C^{1}
with respect to
\mathscr{A}
, we see that
\Phi(t,s)
is well-defined near
(t,s)=(0,0)
. We can compute
\Phi
in terms of
f
.

Clearly
\Phi(t,s)=(*,s)
since
e^{s\partial_{y}}(x,y)=(x,y+s)
. We can write
\Phi(t,s)=(\phi(t,s),s)
. Define
\Phi(t,s;r)
for
r\in\mathbb{R}
as the solution to the ODE
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\Phi(t,s;r)=tX(\Phi(t,s;r))+sY(\Phi(t,s;r))
,
\Phi(t,s;0)=0
. So
\Phi(t,s)=\Phi(t,s;1)
and we have
\Phi(t,s;r)=(\phi(t,s;r),rs)
where

\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\phi(t,s;r)=t+sf(rs)\phi(t,s;r),\quad\phi(t,s;0)=0,\quad t,s,r\in\mathbb{R}.

Solving this ODE we have

\phi(t,s;r)=e^{\int_{0}^{r}sf(\rho s)d\rho}\int_{0}^{r}e^{-\int_{0}^{\rho}sf(\mu s)d\mu}td\rho,\quad\phi(t,s)=\phi(t,s;1)=t\frac{e^{-\int_{0}^{s}f(\rho)d\rho}}{s}\int_{0}^{s}e^{\int_{0}^{\rho}f(\mu)d\mu}d\rho.

Now plug in

f(y)=\begin{cases}\alpha y^{\alpha-1},&y\geq 0,\\
0&y\leq 0.\end{cases}

We have

\phi(t,s)=t\frac{e^{-s^{\alpha}}}{s}\int_{0}^{s}e^{\rho^{\alpha}}d\rho\quad\text{when }s>0;\quad\phi(t,s)=t\quad\text{when }s\leq 0.

Thus,
\phi(t,s)=tg(s)
where

g(s)=\begin{cases}\frac{e^{-s^{\alpha}}}{s}\int_{0}^{s}e^{\rho^{\alpha}}d\rho&s>0\\
1&s\leq 0.\end{cases}

(8.2)

We are going to show
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\Phi\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
near
(0,0)
. To see this, it suffices to show
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
and
g\notin\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
near
0
, for every
\varepsilon>0
.

By Taylor’s expansion on the exponential function we have, when
s>0
,

\displaystyle g(s)

\displaystyle=e^{-s^{\alpha}}\frac{1}{s}\int_{0}^{s}e^{\rho^{\alpha}}d\rho=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}s^{j\alpha}}{j!}\frac{1}{s}\int_{0}^{s}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\rho^{k\alpha}}{k!}d\rho=\sum_{j,k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!k!}\frac{s^{j\alpha}s^{k\alpha}}{k\alpha+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{l}\frac{(-1)^{l-k}}{k\alpha+1}\Big)s^{l\alpha}=1-\big(1-\tfrac{1}{\alpha+1}\big)s^{\alpha}+O(s^{2\alpha}).

In other words

g(s)=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{l}\frac{(-1)^{l-k}}{k\alpha+1}\Big)\max(s,0)^{l\alpha}=1-\big(1-\tfrac{1}{\alpha+1}\big)\max(s,0)^{\alpha}+\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{l}\frac{(-1)^{l-k}}{k\alpha+1}\Big)\max(s,0)^{l\alpha},\quad\forall s\in\mathbb{R}.

Note that for
\beta>0
the function
\max(s,0)^{\beta}=\begin{cases}s^{\beta}&s\geq 0\\
0&s\leq 0\end{cases}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
but not
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+\varepsilon}
near
0
.
Indeed when
0<\beta<1
we know that
\max(s,0)^{\beta}\in C^{0,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and is not
C^{0,\beta+\varepsilon}=\mathscr{C}^{\beta+\varepsilon}
near
0
for any
0<\varepsilon<1-\beta
, when
\beta=1
we know
\max(s,0)\in C^{0,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and is not
C^{1}
near 0 so is not
\mathscr{C}^{1+\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
. For
\beta>1
by passing to its derivatives we see that
\max(s,0)^{\beta}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and is not
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+\varepsilon}
near
0
.

So the remainder
\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}\big(\sum_{k=0}^{l}\frac{(-1)^{l-k}}{k\alpha+1}\big)\max(s,0)^{l\alpha}
is
\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}
for all
0<\varepsilon\leq\alpha
, while the main term
1-\big(1-\tfrac{1}{\alpha+1}\big)\max(s,0)^{\alpha}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
but not
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
near 0. Therefore we conclude that
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
, but
g\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
near
s=0
.

Now we know
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
but
\Phi\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}
near
(t,s)=(0,0)
. Consider the inverse function of
\Phi
,
and set
(u(x,y),v(x,y)):=\Phi^{-1}(x,y)
; so that
\Phi^{*}Y=(Yu,Yv)\circ\Phi
. We have
v(x,y)=y
and
u(x,y)=\frac{1}{g(y)}x
. Note that
g(s)>0
for every
s
, so
y\mapsto\frac{1}{g(y)}
is not
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
near
y=0
, for any
\varepsilon>0
. Therefore
Yv=x\cdot\partial_{y}\frac{1}{g(y)}
is not
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon-1}
near
(x,y)=(0,0)
. By composing with
\Phi
which is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-diffeomorphism (for
0<\varepsilon<1
), we see that
\Phi^{*}Y
is not
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon-1}
near
(t,s)=(0,0)
, for every
\varepsilon>0
. ∎

Remark 8.7
.

As a differentiable map
\Phi:(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{t,s},\mathrm{std})\to(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathscr{A})
between two
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifolds, we see that
\Phi
is not
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
near
(0,0)
for any
\varepsilon>0
. Otherwise since
X
and
Y
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
on
\mathscr{A}
, by Lemma
4.1

(ii)
we have
\Phi^{*}Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\alpha-1+\varepsilon)}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1+\min(\varepsilon,1)}
near
(0,0)
, contradicting to Lemma
8.6
.

9
The Quantitative Result

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be an
n
-dimensional manifold and let
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
. If we are given
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{q}
that span the tangent space at every point, we can write
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{q}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
for some
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-functions
c_{ij}^{k}
. In Theorem
3.1
we show that, for
s_{0}>\alpha-1
and near each point
p
the following are equivalent

•

There exists a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-parameterization
\Phi
near
p
such that
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}
are
\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

•

We may choose
c_{ij}^{k}
with
c_{ij}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}
near
p
.

By contrast the range of
s_{0}
in
[
Str21
]
is
s_{0}>1
.

If one traces through the proof of Theorem
3.1
, the size of the neighborhood of
p
and the
\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}
-norms of
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}
depend on the
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-norms of
X_{1},\dots,X_{q}
under some fixed initial coordinate system near
p
, and on a lower
bound for (
1.1
) at
x=p
(in some fixed initial coordinate
system).
However, in
[
Str21
]
, when
X_{1},\dots,X_{q}\in C^{1}
and
s_{0}>1
, a similar coordinate system
\Phi
was constructed, but where all of the estimates depend only on the diffeomorphic invariant quantities like the norms
\|c_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}}_{X}}
(see
[
SS18
, Section 5.1]
).

Using the methods of this paper, we can extend the main results of

[
Str21
]
and
[
Str20b
]
(namely,

[
Str21
, Theorem 2.14]
and
[
Str20b
, Theorem 4.5]
) from
s_{0}>1
to
s_{0}>0
.

Theorem 9.1
.

[
Str21
, Theorem 2.14]
and
[
Str20b
, Theorem 4.5]
are still true with
s_{0}>1
replaced by
s_{0}>0
, and leaving rest of the assumptions and statements unchanged.

In these papers,
the assumption
s_{0}>1
is used in the following places:

•

In
[
SS18
, Theorem 4.7]
, which is used to prove

[
Str21
, Proposition 4.1]
,
“1-admissible constants” are used in order to obtain the results (d), (e), and (f) of
[
SS18
, Theorem 4.7]
. The proof of
[
SS18
, Theorem 4.7 (d), (e), (f)]
is done in
[
SS18
, Proposition 9.22]
.
The 1-admissible constants are allowed to depend on quantities like
\|c_{ij}^{k}\|_{C^{1}_{X}}
.

We are going to show that in
[
SS18
, Proposition 9.22]
, if we only need the conclusion that
\Phi
is a
\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}
-diffeomorphism, then the assumption “1-admissible constants” can be replaced by “
\{s_{0}\}
-admissible constants,”
for a fixed
s_{0}>0
(which is possibly
\leq 1
), where
\{s_{0}\}
-admissible constants are defined
in
[
Str21
, Definition 2.13]
. See Lemma
9.8
.

•

In
[
Str21
, Proposition 6.8]
, the assumption
s_{0}>1
is used in order to set up some well-defined elliptic PDEs.

In this paper we use different elliptic PDEs that are defined when
0<s_{0}\leq 1
, as illustrated in Section
5.9
. See Proposition
9.4
for the precise statement to the modification of
[
Str21
, Proposition 6.8]
.

•

In
[
Str21
, Theorem 2.14]
, a map
\Phi

is constructed, which depends on
s_{0}
, such that
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{q}\in\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}
.
Moreover, this map satisfies for
s\geq s_{0}
, that if

c_{ij}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{s}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}
, then
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{s+1}
, with appropriate bounds on their
\mathscr{C}^{s+1}
norms.
In
[
Str21
, Theorem 2.14]
, these estimates required

s\geq s_{0}>1
; but by using the regularity theory of elliptic
PDEs we will be able to extend this to
s\geq s_{0}>0
.

•

Once we have established
[
Str21
, Theorem 2.14 (a)-(j)]

for
s_{0}>0
, the proof from
[
Str21
]

of
[
Str21
, Theorem 2.14 (k) and (l)]
also establishes
these results for
s_{0}>0
.

•

The only place that
s_{0}>1
is used in
[
Str20b
, Theorem 4.5]
is when it refers to
[
Str21
, Theorem 2.14]
. Once
[
Str21
, Theorem 2.14]
is established for
s_{0}>0
, the same is true of
[
Str20b
, Theorem 4.5]
.

[
Str21
, Theorem 2.14]

begins with

X_{1},\dots,X_{q}
which are
C^{1}
-vector fields on
\mathfrak{M}
such that
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{q}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
for some
c_{ij}^{k}\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
. By passing to an immersed submanifold using
[
SS18
, Proposition 3.1]
we may assume that
X_{1},\dots,X_{q}
span the tangent space at every point.

Fix a point
p\in\mathfrak{M}
. We choose
J_{0}=(j^{0}_{1},\dots,j^{0}_{n})\in\{1,\dots,q\}^{n}
such that
X_{j^{0}_{1}}(p)\wedge\dots X_{j^{0}_{n}}(p)\neq 0
and

\max_{1\leq j_{1}<\dots<j_{n}\leq q}\left|\frac{X_{j_{1}}(p)\wedge\dots\wedge X_{j_{n}}(p)}{X_{j^{0}_{1}}(p)\wedge\dots\wedge X_{j^{0}_{n}}(p)}\right|\leq\zeta^{-1}.

(9.1)

Here
\zeta>0
is a constant which all of our estimates may depend on; one
can always pick
j_{1}^{0},\ldots,j_{n}^{0}
so that the left hand side of (
9.1
)
equals
1
, though it is convenient in some applications to allow for
\zeta<1
.

In
[
SS18
, Definition 4.1]
“0-admissible constants” are defined to be constants that depend only on diffeomorphic invariant quantities like
\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{q}\|c_{ij}^{k}\|_{C^{0}(B_{X_{J_{0}}}(p,\xi))}
where
\xi>0
is a small, given constant
on which our estimates may depend,
and
X_{J_{0}}=(X_{j_{1}^{0}},\ldots,X_{j_{n}^{0}})
. See
[
SS18
, Definition 4.1]
for the
precise definition.

Fix
s_{0}>0
. For
s\geq s_{0}
we define
\{s\}
-admissible constants as in

[
Str21
, Definition 2.13]
except we only require
s_{0}>0
rather than
s_{0}>1
.
These are constants which depend only on diffeomorphic invariant quantities like

\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{q}\|c_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}_{X_{J_{0}}}(C^{0}(B_{X_{J_{0}}}(p,\xi)))}
.
See
[
Str21
, Definition 2.13]
for the precise definition.

Recall that
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}
span the tangent space to
\mathfrak{M}
at every point.
Moreover, by reordering
X_{1},\ldots,X_{q}
so that
j_{1}^{0}=1,\ldots,j_{n}^{0}=n
,
we may assume that
X_{1}(p),\ldots,X_{n}(p)
form a basis for
T_{p}\mathfrak{M}

and (
9.1
) holds with
X_{1}(p)\wedge\cdots\wedge X_{n}(p)

in the denominator.

We begin by considering the canonical coordinates

\Phi_{0}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}):=e^{x_{1}X_{1}+\dots+x_{n}X_{n}}(p)
. In the following lemma we prove an analog of
[
Str21
, Proposition 4.1]
when
s_{0}>0
(as opposed to
s_{0}>1
).
In this case, we only show that
\Phi_{0}
is locally a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism
rather than globally a
C^{2}
-diffeomorphism. In particular, we only
show that
\Phi_{0}
is locally injective.

Lemma 9.2
.

There is an
\{s_{0}\}
-admissible constant
7
7

7

In
[
SS18
]
and
[
Str21
]

a constant similar to
\mu_{0}
was called
\eta_{0}
.

\mu_{0}>0
, such that
\Phi_{0}(x):=e^{x\cdot X}(p)
is defined for
x\in B^{n}(\mu_{0})
and
\Phi_{0}:B^{n}(\mu_{0})\to\mathfrak{M}

is a locally
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism, so that we can pullback
X_{1},\dots,X_{q}
to
B^{n}(\mu_{0})
. Moreover, by writing
Y_{j}=\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,q
and
[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}]^{\top}=(I+A)\nabla
, we have

(i)

A(0)=0
,
\sup\limits_{x\in B^{n}(\mu_{0})}|A(x)|_{\mathbb{M}^{n\times n}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0});\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
for
s\geq s_{0}
.

(ii)

There exist
b_{k}^{l}\in\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))
,
1\leq l\leq n<k\leq q
, such that
Y_{k}=\sum_{l=1}^{n}b_{k}^{l}Y_{l}
for
n+1\leq k\leq q
. Moreover
\displaystyle\sum_{k=n+1}^{q}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\|b_{k}^{l}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s+1}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
.

(iii)

There exist
\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))
for
1\leq i,j\leq q
and
1\leq k\leq n
, such that
\Phi_{0}^{*}[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\cdot Y_{k}
on
B^{n}(\mu_{0})
for
1\leq i,j\leq q
. Moreover
\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{q}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
.

Remark 9.3
.

(a)

The constant
\mu_{0}
does not depend on
\|c_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}}}
. It is almost a 0-admissible constant (see
[
SS18
, Definition 4.1]
) except it also depends on the quantity
\eta>0
in
[
SS18
, Section 3.2]
.

(b)

When
\Phi_{0}
is locally
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism, for any (continuous) vector field
L
on
\mathfrak{M}
there is a unique vector field
\tilde{L}
on
B^{n}(\mu_{0})
such that
d\Phi_{0}(x)\tilde{L}\big|_{x}=L\big|_{x}
. So we define the pullback vector field as
\tilde{L}:=\Phi_{0}^{*}L
.

(c)

We cannot say
[Y_{i},Y_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}Y_{k}
yet, since
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{q}
are
\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}}
and we may not be able to talk about commutators of
\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}}
-vector fields when
0<s_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Nevertheless
[Y_{i},Y_{j}]
can be thought of as
\Phi_{0}^{*}[X_{i},X_{j}]
.

(d)

When
0<s<1
, by standard results from ODEs we only know that
\Phi_{0}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{1+s}
-map and we do not expect
\Phi_{0}
to be
C^{2}
.
Unfortunately, the proof of injectivity for
\Phi_{0}
in
[
SS18
, Proposition 9.15]

requires
\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{n}
to be
C^{1}

Nevertheless we will show that
\Phi_{0}
is injective when restricted to a ball centered at 0 with a smaller radius, and this smaller radius is a
\{s_{0}\}
-admissible constant. See Lemma
9.8
and Remark
9.9
.

(e)

Lemma
9.2
“loses one derivative”
in the sense that it implies
\|Y_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
, but our main result
gives
\|Y_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s+1}}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
.
Similar to the proof in
[
Str21
]
, we will recover this lost derivative
by composing with another map
\Phi_{1}
in Proposition
9.4
(see also
[
Str21
, Proposition 6.3]
).

Proof of Lemma
9.2
.

Let
\tilde{\eta}>0
be a number such that
\Phi_{0}
is defined on
B^{n}(\tilde{\eta})
and
\Phi_{0}
cannot be defined on
B^{n}(\tilde{\eta}^{\prime})
for any
\tilde{\eta}^{\prime}>\tilde{\eta}
. Note that
\tilde{\eta}
is bounded below by a positive
\{s_{0}\}
-admissible constant (also see
[
SS18
, Definitions 3.7 and 3.10]
).

We first prove the result in the special case
q=n
. In this case,

X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
form a basis of the tangent space to
\mathfrak{M}
at every point. Thus,
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
where
(c_{ij}^{k})_{i,j,k=1}^{n}
are uniquely determined by
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
.

By
[
SS18
, Lemma 9.6]
we know there is a unique
8
8

8

In
[
SS18
, Section 9.3.1]
, what we call
\tilde{A}
is called
A
, and what we call

A
is called
\widehat{A}
.

\tilde{A}\in C^{0}(B^{n}(0,\tilde{\eta});\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})
such that

\begin{gathered}\begin{cases}\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(r\tilde{A}(r\theta))=-\tilde{A}(r\theta)^{2}-C(r\theta)\tilde{A}(r\theta)-C(r\theta),&\text{for }|r|<\tilde{\eta}\text{ and }\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1},\\
\tilde{A}(0)=0,\end{cases}\\
\text{ where }C(x)_{i}^{j}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}x_{k}\cdot c_{ik}^{j}(\Phi_{0}(x)),\quad x\in B^{n}(0,\tilde{\eta}),\quad 1\leq i,j\leq n.\end{gathered}

(9.2)

By
[
SS18
, Proposition 9.4]
there is a
0
-admissible constant
D>0
(in fact, depending only on
n
and upper bounds for
\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}\|c_{ij}^{k}\|_{C^{0}(\mathfrak{M})}
), such that (see (
4.17
) in Convention
4.11
)

|\tilde{A}(x)|_{\mathbb{M}^{n\times n}}\leq D|x|,\quad x\in B^{n}(0,\tilde{\eta}).

(9.3)

Take
\mu_{0}:=\frac{1}{2D}
, so
\|\tilde{A}\|_{C^{0}(B^{n}(\mu_{0});\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Therefore
I+A(x)
is invertible matrix at every point
x\in B^{n}(\mu_{0})
, which means
\Phi_{0}
has non-degenerate tangent map at every point. By the Inverse Function Theorem,
\Phi_{0}
is a locally
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism.

Define the matrix
A
by
Y=:(I+A)\nabla
; where we are treating

Y
as the column vector of vector fields
Y=[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}]^{\top}
,
and
\nabla
is thought of as a column vector.
It follows from
[
SS18
, Proposition 9.18]
, that
A=\tilde{A}
in
B^{n}(\mu_{0})
and
\|\tilde{A}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0});\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
. Since
Y=(I+A)\nabla
, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|Y_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0});\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
, finishing the proof of
(i)
when
n=q
.

Since
\|A\|_{C^{0}(B^{n}(\mu_{0});\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\leq\frac{1}{2}
, we have
\|(I+A)^{-1}\|_{C^{0}(B^{n}(\mu_{0});\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2})^{k}=\frac{3}{2}<\infty
, so by
[
Str21
, Lemma 5.7]
(applied to the cofactor representation of
(I+A)^{-1}
) with
\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0});\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
, we get

\|(I+A)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}+\|(I+A)^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1.

(9.4)

Take
\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}:=c_{ij}^{k}\circ\Phi_{0}
for
1\leq i,j,k\leq n
. We separate the proof of
\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
into the case
s>1
and the case
0<s\leq 1
.

When
s>1
, we have
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{s}\subset C^{1}
. By
[
SS18
, Lemma 9.24]
we have
\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
.

When
0<s\leq 1
, we may not have
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}\in C^{1}
. In order to use previous results such as
[
SS18
, Proposition 8.6]
, we need
\Phi_{0}
to be (qualitatively)
C^{2}
; though we do not require any estimates on any
C^{2}
norm of
\Phi_{0}
. To get around the fact that
\Phi_{0}
is not
C^{2}
, we introduce another atlas on
B^{n}(\mu_{0})
with respect to which
\Phi_{0}
is
C^{2}
. Indeed, we say
f:B^{n}(\mu_{0})\to\mathbb{R}
is
C^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
with respect to the atlas
\mathscr{A}
, if for every open subset
U\subseteq B^{n}(\mu_{0})
such that
\Phi_{0}:U\to\Phi_{0}(U)
is bijective, we have
f\circ\Phi_{0}^{-1}:\Phi_{0}(U)\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}
is
C^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

Since
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}
are
\mathscr{C}^{s}
and span the tangent space at every point in
B^{n}(\mu_{0})
, we know
\mathscr{A}
is compatible with the standard
\mathscr{C}^{s+1}
-structure on
B^{n}(\mu_{0})
. In particular both
(B^{n}(\mu_{0}),\mathscr{A})

agrees with the standard
C^{1,s/2}
-structure on
B^{n}(\mu_{0})
.

Now
\Phi_{0}:(B^{n}(\mu_{0}),\mathscr{A})\to\mathfrak{M}
is
C^{2}
. By
[
SS18
, Proposition 8.6]
we have

\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}_{Y}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}),\mathscr{A})}=\|c_{ij}^{k}\circ\Phi_{0}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}_{Y}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}),\mathscr{A})}\leq\|c_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}_{X}(\mathfrak{M})}\quad 1\leq i,j,k\leq n,\quad 0<s\leq 1.

(9.5)

On the other hand by
[
SS18
, Section 2.2]
, the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}_{Y}}
involves only the
C^{1,s/2}
-structure of the manifold. Indeed for
0<s\leq 1
, on the domain
U=B^{n}(\mu_{0})
,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}_{Y}}=\|f\|_{C^{0}}+\sup\limits_{x,y\in U;x\neq y}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{\operatorname{dist}_{Y}(x,y)^{\frac{s}{2}}}+\sup\limits_{\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{Y,\frac{s}{2}}(h)}h^{-s}|f(\gamma(2h))-2f(\gamma(h))+f(\gamma(0))|,\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{Y,\frac{s}{2}}(h)=\Big\{\gamma\in C^{1,\frac{s}{2}}([0,2h];U):\dot{\gamma}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}(t)Y_{j}(\gamma(t)),\ d_{1},\dots,d_{n}\in C^{0,\frac{s}{2}}[0,2h],\ \sum_{j=1}^{n}\|d_{j}\|_{C^{0,\frac{s}{2}}[0,2h]}^{2}\leq 1\Big\},

\displaystyle\operatorname{dist}_{Y}(x,y)=\inf\Big\{T>0:\exists\gamma\in C^{0,1}([0,T];U),\gamma(0)=x,\gamma(T)=y,\dot{\gamma}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}d_{j}(t)Y_{j}(\gamma(t)),\ \sum_{j=1}^{n}\|d_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}[0,T]}^{2}\leq 1\Big\}.

Here
\operatorname{dist}_{Y}
only depends on the Lipschitz structure of
B^{n}(\mu_{0})
and
\mathcal{P}_{Y,\frac{s}{2}}(h)
only depends on the
C^{1,\frac{s}{2}}
-structure of
B^{n}(\mu_{0})
.

Since
\mathscr{A}
is compatible with the standard
C^{1,\frac{s}{2}}
-structure for
B^{n}(\mu_{0})
, we have

\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}_{Y}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}),\mathscr{A})}=\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}_{Y}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))},\quad 1\leq i,j,k\leq n,\quad 0<s\leq 1.

(9.6)

To show
\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
, it remains to show that
\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}\lesssim_{\{s\}}\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}_{Y}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}
.

Note that
\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))\subset C^{0,s/2}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))
with
\|f\|_{C^{0,s/2}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}\lesssim_{n,s,\mu_{0}}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}
for all
f
. Using (
9.4
) and
[
SS18
, Proposition 8.12]
we get
\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}\lesssim_{\{s-1\}}\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}_{Y}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}
, in particular
\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}\lesssim_{\{s\}}\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}_{Y}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}
.
Here the implicit
\{s-1\}
-admissible constant (that appears when we say “
\lesssim_{\{s-1\}}
”) is given in
[
SS18
, Definition 8.10]
, which depends on the upper bound of
C^{0,\frac{s}{2}}
-norms of
(\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k})_{i,j,k=1}^{n}
.

Combining this with (
9.5
) and (
9.6
) we get
\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
for
0<s\leq 1
, finishing the proof of
(iii)
when
q=n
.

The general case
q>n
can be reduced to the case
q=n
as in
[
SS18
, Section 9.3.2]
: we get
(ii)
from
[
SS18
, Lemma 9.33]
and
(iii)
from
[
SS18
, Lemma 9.34]
.
∎

Lemma
9.2
yields a local
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism
\Phi_{0}:B^{n}(\mu_{0})\to\mathfrak{M}
and
\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\in C^{0}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))
for
1\leq i,j,k\leq n
such that
\Phi_{0}^{*}[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}Y_{k}
for
1\leq i,j\leq n
and
\sum_{i=1}^{q}\|\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0});\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(\mu_{0}))}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
for
s\geq s_{0}
.
Thus, we have reduced the problem to studying vector fields on
B^{n}(\mu_{0})
,
which have estimates in terms of classical function spaces, instead of the
abstract function spaces
\mathscr{C}^{s}_{X}
.

By Lemma
9.2

(ii)
,
\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{n+1},\dots,\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{q}
are now linear combinations of
\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{n}
whose coefficients have
\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}
-norms bounded by a
\{s_{0}\}
-admissible constant. So we can assume
q=n
, just as in the beginning of
[
Str21
, Section 6]
.

When
s_{0}>1
, the proof of
[
Str21
, Theorem 2.14]
is as follow.
In
[
Str21
, Proposition 6.3]
it is shown
that there is a map
\Phi_{2}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to B^{n}(\mu_{0})
which is
\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}
-diffeomorphism onto its image such that
\Phi_{2}^{*}Y_{1},\dots,\Phi_{2}^{*}Y_{n}
are
\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}
and have
\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}
-norms bounded by a
\{s_{0}\}
-admissible constant. Meanwhile if
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}
and
(\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k})_{i,j,k=1}^{n}
are all
\mathscr{C}^{s}
for some
s>s_{0}
, then
\Phi_{2}
is automatically
\mathscr{C}^{s+1}
and the
\mathscr{C}^{s+1}
-norms of the coefficients of
\Phi_{2}^{*}Y_{1},\dots,\Phi_{2}^{*}Y_{n}
are automatically bounded by a
\{s\}
-admissible constant. This completes the proof when
s_{0}>1
. Our goal
is to generalize this argument to
s_{0}>0
.

Based on the techniques from Section
5
, we can prove an analog of
[
Str21
, Proposition 6.3]
in the setting of
s_{0}>0
. We formulate the statement in
the proposition below.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be
C^{1}
-vector fields on a
C^{2}
-manifold
\mathfrak{M}
that form a basis of the tangent space at every point. Near a fixed point
p\in\mathfrak{M}
we define
\Phi_{0}(x):=e^{x\cdot X}(p)
. Write
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
where
c_{ij}^{k}\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
are uniquely determined by
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
.

On the subset of the domain of
\Phi_{0}
where
\nabla\Phi_{0}
is non-degenerate (so
\Phi_{0}
is locally
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism on this set), we denote
Y_{i}:=\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{i}
and
\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}:=\Phi_{0}^{*}c_{ij}^{k}
for
1\leq i,j,k\leq n
. And we write
Y=[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}]^{\top}
as
Y=(I+A(x))\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
where
A
is a
\mathbb{M}^{n\times n}
-valued function defined on the domain of
\Phi_{0}
.

Proposition 9.4
.

Let
s_{0},\mu_{0}>0
,
s\geq s_{0}
and
M_{0},M_{1}>0
. There are constants
\widehat{K}=\widehat{K}(n,s_{0},\mu_{0},M_{0})>0
,
K_{0}=K_{0}(n,s_{0},\mu_{0},M_{0})>0
, and
K_{1}=K_{1}(n,s_{0},s,\mu_{0},M_{0},M_{1})>0
that satisfy the following:

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
and
\Phi_{0}(x)=e^{x\cdot X}(p)
be as above. Suppose we have the following:

•

\Phi_{0}:B^{n}(0,\mu_{0})\to\mathfrak{M}
is defined and is a locally
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism onto its image (so that
Y,A,\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}
are defined on
B^{n}(0,\mu_{0})
).

•

\sup\limits_{|x|<\mu_{0}}|A(x)|\leq\frac{1}{2}
and

\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}}(B^{n}(0,\mu_{0});\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}+\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}}(B^{n}(0,\mu_{0}))}<M_{0}.

(9.7)

Then

(i)

There is a map
\Phi_{1}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to B^{n}(0,\mu_{0})
such that

•

\Phi_{1}(0)=0
and
\Phi_{1}
is
\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}
-diffeomorphism onto its image.

•

\Phi_{1}^{*}Y=[\Phi_{1}^{*}Y_{1},\dots,\Phi_{1}^{*}Y_{n}]^{\top}
is a collection of
\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}
-vector fields on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
that can be written as

\Phi_{1}^{*}Y=\widehat{K}(I+\widehat{A})\nabla,\quad\text{where }\widehat{A}(0)=0,\ \|\widehat{A}\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}.

(9.8)

Moreover, we have the estimate

\|\Phi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|\widehat{A}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\leq K_{0}.

(9.9)

(ii)

Suppose additionally
A
and
\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}
are all
\mathscr{C}^{s}
with

\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(0,\mu_{0});\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}+\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}\|\tilde{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(0,\mu_{0}))}<M_{1}.

(9.10)

Then
\Phi_{1}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to B^{n}(0,\mu_{0})
is a
\mathscr{C}^{s+1}
-map and
\widehat{A}\in\mathscr{C}^{s+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})
. Moreover

\|\Phi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq K_{1},\qquad\|\widehat{A}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\leq K_{1}.

(9.11)

Remark 9.5
.

(a)

For the proof of Theorem
9.1
we will apply Proposition
9.4
with
\Phi_{0}

and
\mu_{0}

as in Lemma
9.2
. In this application,

\widehat{K}
and
K_{0}
are
\{s_{0}\}
-admissible constants and
K_{1}
is an
\{s\}
-admissible constant.
In particular, in this application, we have
\|\Phi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s+1}}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
and
\|\widehat{A}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s+1}}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
.

(b)

A map similar to
\Phi_{1}
in Proposition
9.4
is called
\Phi_{2}
in
[
Str21
, Proposition 6.3]
. In the proof of
[
Str21
, Proposition 6.3]
,
\Phi_{2}
is decomposed as a
\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}
-diffeomorphism and a scaling map. In our setting the scaling is already done
in Proposition
5.15
. Also see Lemma
9.7
.

We need some preliminary results to prove Proposition
9.4
.

Suppose we have Proposition
9.4

(i)
, that is, we construct a
\Phi_{1}
such that
\|\Phi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\Phi_{1}^{*}Y_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}}\lesssim_{\{s_{0}\}}1
. Note that the
\Phi_{1}
does not depend on the index
s
. In order to prove Proposition
9.4

(ii)
, i.e.
\|\Phi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s+1}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\Phi_{1}^{*}Y_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s+1}}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
for every
s>s_{0}
(see also for
[
Str21
, Theorem 2.14 (j)]
), we need to give regularity estimate for Theorem
5.1
.

Instead of vector fields, we proceed by using 1-forms.

Recall in Section
5.9
we start with 1-forms
\lambda^{i}=dx^{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}^{i}dx^{j}
,
i=1,\dots,n
, defined on
\mathbb{B}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
A=(a_{j}^{i})_{n\times n}
is supported in
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
. We let
F=\mathrm{id}+R:\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x}\to\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y}

be the map in Proposition
5.10
, which is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism and solves (
5.8
) with
R\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
. We write the pushforward 1-forms
\eta^{i}=F_{*}\lambda^{i}
,
i=1,\dots,n
on
\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y}
as
\eta^{i}=dy^{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}^{i}dy^{j}
. By Lemma
5.13
we know
B=(b_{j}^{i})_{n\times n}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{M}^{n\times n}
solves (
5.9
), which can be rewritten as (
5.27
).

Proposition 9.6
.

Fix
s_{0}>0
. There is a
c^{\prime}=c^{\prime}(n,s_{0})>0
, such that in additional to the results in Theorem
5.1
with
\alpha=s_{0}
,
\beta=s_{0}+1
and
c(n,s_{0},s_{0}+1)=c^{\prime}(n,s_{0})
, we have the following:

(i)

For the collection of 1-forms
[F_{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,F_{*}\lambda^{n}]^{\top}=(I+B)dy
we have
\|B\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}\leq\frac{1}{4}
.

And for any
s\geq s_{0}
,
M^{\prime}>0
, there is a
K^{\prime}=K^{\prime}(n,s,s_{0},M^{\prime})>0
such that if in addition to the assumptions of Theorem
5.1
, we have
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n},d\lambda^{1},\dots,d\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{s}
with

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\lambda^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|d\lambda^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}<M^{\prime},

then:

(ii)

The map
F:\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x}\to\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y}
in Theorem
5.1
is
\mathscr{C}^{s+1}
and its inverse
\Phi
satisfies
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<K^{\prime}
.

(iii)

The 1-forms
\eta^{1},\dots,\eta^{n}
are all
\mathscr{C}^{s+1}
and the coefficient matrix
B:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{M}^{n\times n}
satisfies
\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s+1}(\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}<K^{\prime}
.

Informally Proposition
9.6

(iii)
is saying
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\eta^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s+1}}\lesssim_{\{s\}}1
.

Note that we require
\|\lambda\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}}}
and
\|d\lambda\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}}}
to be small (bounded by the constant
c
from Theorem
5.1
) in the assumption of
Proposition
9.6
.
However, by taking
M^{\prime}
large in Proposition
9.6
, we can allow
\|\lambda\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}}
and
\|d\lambda\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}}
to be large for
s>s_{0}
.

Proof.

For
(i)
, let
c=c(n,s_{0},s_{0}+1)>0
be the original constant in Theorem
5.1
. By (
5.2
) we see that for any
c^{\prime}\in(0,c]
,
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\lambda^{i}-dx^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}}}+\|d\lambda^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}}}<c^{\prime}
implies
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|F_{*}\lambda^{i}-dy^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq\frac{c^{\prime}}{c}
.

Recall the notation
F_{*}\lambda=(I+B)dy
. Since
\|B\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim_{s_{0}}\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}}\approx_{s_{0}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|F_{*}\lambda^{i}-dy^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}}
, by choosing
c^{\prime}\in(0,c]
small enough we can ensure
\|B\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})}<\frac{1}{4}
.

To prove
(ii)
and
(iii)
, we choose
c^{\prime}(n,s_{0})\in(0,c]

as follows.

Let
c_{1}=c_{1}(n,s_{0},s_{0}+1)>0
,
c_{2}=c_{2}(n,s_{0},s_{0}+1)>0
, and
c_{3}=c_{3}(n,s_{0},s_{0}+1)>0
be the constants in Proposition
5.10
, Lemma
5.13
, and Proposition
5.14
, respectively.

In the proof of Theorem
5.1
we see that if
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\lambda^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}}}+\|d\lambda^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}}}<\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\min(c_{1},c_{2}c_{3})
then
B\in\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})\subset C^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{M}^{n\times n})
. We are going to find a smaller constant
c_{3}^{\prime}(n,s_{0})\in(0,c_{3})
and then take
c^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\min(c_{1},c_{2}c_{3}^{\prime})
.

Recall
\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(B)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}(\sqrt{\det h}h^{ij}-\delta^{ij})b_{j}^{k}
,
1\leq i,k\leq n
in (
5.27
) (see (
5.7
) for
h^{ij}
and
\sqrt{\det h}
) are rational functions which are finite near the origin such that
\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(B)=O(|B|^{2})
near
B=0\in\mathbb{M}^{n\times n}
(see (
5.5
), (
5.7
), and Lemma
5.3
). So when
B\in C^{1}
, we can write
\partial_{y^{i}}\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(B)=\sum_{j,l=0}^{n}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{il}^{kj}(B)\cdot\partial_{y^{i}}b_{j}^{l}
where

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{il}^{kl}(v):=\frac{\partial\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}}{\partial v_{j}^{l}}(v),\quad\text{for }1\leq i,j,k,l\leq n,\quad\text{defined for }v\in\mathbb{M}^{n\times n}\text{ closed to }0.

So
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{il}^{kl}(0)=0
since
|\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(v)|\lesssim|v|^{2}
for small
v
. The equation (
5.27
) can be rewritten as

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}b_{i}^{k}+\sum_{i,j,l=0}^{n}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{il}^{jk}(B)\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}b_{j}^{l}=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y},\quad k=1,\dots,n.

(9.12)

By the conclusions of Proposition
5.14
we have
B\in\mathscr{C}^{s_{0}+1}\subset C^{1}
. And by the assumptions of Proposition
5.14
combined
with (
9.12
), we know
u=B
is a
C^{1}
-solution to the following system of equations in
u=(u_{i}^{j})_{n\times n}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial u_{k}^{i}}{\partial y^{j}}-\frac{\partial u_{j}^{i}}{\partial y^{k}}=d\eta^{i}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}},\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{k}}\Big),

\displaystyle i=1,\dots,n,\quad 1\leq j<k\leq n.

(9.13)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}u_{i}^{k}+\sum_{i,j,l=0}^{n}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{il}^{jk}(B)\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}u_{j}^{l}=0,

\displaystyle k=1,\dots,n.

(
9.13
) is of the form

\mathcal{E}u+\mathcal{L}_{B}u=g_{B},

where
\mathcal{E}:C^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}})\to C^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\frac{n^{2}-n+2}{2}})
is a first order constant linear differential operator that does not depend on
B
, and
\mathcal{L}_{B}
is a first order linear differential operator with coefficients comes from
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{il}^{jk}(B)
. Here,
g_{B}
is the vector-valued function which is the right hand side of (
9.13
); i.e.,
g_{B}=(d\eta^{1},\dots,d\eta^{n},0)
.

If we write
u^{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}u^{i}_{j}dy^{j}
,
i=1,\dots,n
, then we see that
\mathcal{E}(u^{i}_{j})=(du^{i},\vartheta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}u^{i})_{i=1}^{n}
. So
\mathcal{E}^{*}\mathcal{E}=d\vartheta+\vartheta d=\bigtriangleup
is elliptic, which implies that
\mathcal{E}
is an elliptic operator.

By classical elliptic theory (see
[
Str21
, Proposition A.1]
) there is a
\gamma=\gamma(\mathcal{E})>0
such that if
\sum_{i,j,k,l=1}^{n}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{il}^{jk}(B)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}<\gamma
then
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{il}^{jk}(B),g_{B}\in\mathscr{C}^{s}
implies
